Visibility Reasoning for Concurrent Snapshot Algorithms Journal: PACMPL Volume: 1 CONF 1 1 CCS: Theory of computation Program verification CCS: Theory of computation Concurrent algorithms
Joakim Öhman, Aleksandar Nanevski

Abstract
. Visibility relations have been proposed by Henzinger et al. as an abstraction for proving linearizability of concurrent algorithms that obtains modular and reusable proofs. This is in contrast to the customary approach based on exhibiting the algorithm’s linearization points. In this paper we apply visibility relations to develop modular proofs for three elegant concurrent snapshot algorithms of Jayanti. The proofs are divided by signatures into components of increasing level of abstraction; the components at higher abstraction levels are shared, i.e., they apply to all three algorithms simultaneously. Importantly, the interface properties mathematically capture Jayanti’s original intuitions that have previously been given only informally.



1

Extended Version

1.
Introduction

Linearizability
(
Herlihy and Wing 1990
)
is a standard correctness condition
for concurrent data structures. It requires that the operations in any
execution over the data structure may be ordered sequentially, without
incurring changes to the observed results. In other words, the methods
of a linearizable concurrent structure exhibit the same external
behavior as the sequential equivalent. Programmers can use the
concurrent variant to efficiently utilize modern systems’ multi-core
setup, and rely on the sequential variant for understanding and formal
reasoning.

Many methods exist for proving linearizability of a data
structure. The standard idea shared by most of them involves finding,
for each method of the structure, a point in time in the concurrent
execution when the method can be considered as
logically

occurring. In other words, a method may execute over a period of time,
admitting interference from other concurrent threads; nevertheless,
for all reasoning intents and purposes, the execution is
indistinguishable from one where the method executes atomically at a
single point in time, without any interference.
This point in time is referred to as the
linearization point
.
Where the linearization point lies for a given method may depend on
the run-time behavior and interleaving of other methods executing
concurrently, sometimes even including behavior that occurs after the
original method has already terminated (the latter are often termed

far future
linearization points). Although significant progress
has been made recently in the verification of concurrent structures
and algorithms, and in particular by the introduction of so-called

prophecy
variables

(
Abadi and Lamport 1991
;
Lynch and
Vaandrager 1995
;
Jacobs
et al. 2018
;
Jung et al. 2020
)
to
model the dependence of linearization points on run-time behavior
(including in the far future), establishing linearizability by
explicitly exhibiting the linearization points of an algorithm remains
a highly complex task in general.

A different approach, which avoids explicit reasoning about
linearization points, has been proposed
by
Henzinger
et al. 2013
.
In this approach, one first specifies a set of properties and proves
that the properties hold of every execution history over the data
structure. One then constructs a proof of linearizability using only
the specified properties as axioms, thereby abstracting from the
underlying executions. In other words, the low-level reasoning about
the concrete placement of linearization points is replaced by
higher-level reasoning out of data structure axioms, in turn
facilitating proof decomposition, modularity, abstraction, and reuse.

The properties used in the axiomatization are defined under relations
tying an event in the execution to other events that depend on it,
i.e., that
observe
it.
For example, the event of reading of a pointer will be related to the
write event that was responsible for mutating the pointer. Once the
pointer is mutated by another write, the subsequent reads will observe
(i.e., be related to) the new write, or possibly another later write.
Similar ideas of reasoning about event observations have been used in
the axiomatizations of weak memory models
(
Raad et al. 2019
)
and in
distributed systems
(
Viotti and
Vukolić 2016
)
where they have been
captured by means of so-called
visibility
relations.
Reasoning by visibility has also been applied in different ways to
prove linearizability of concrete data structures, such as
queues
(
Henzinger
et al. 2013
)
and stacks
(
Dodds
et al. 2015
)
,
including reasoning automation
(
Bouajjani et al. 2017
)
, and reasoning about
relaxed notions of linearizability
(
Emmi and Enea 2019
;
Krishna
et al. 2020
)
.

In this paper, we demonstrate the further applicability of reasoning
by visibility, by applying it in a novel way to even more nuanced
algorithms and proofs. In particular, we show how visibility can be
used to express and axiomatize the important
internal
properties
shared by several
snapshot
algorithms. Similarly to the
approach of
Henzinger
et al. 2013
to queues, the axiomatization
enables a modularization of the linearizability proof: a significant
portion of the proof is carried out once, and then reused for each
snapshot algorithm.

More specifically, we verify the three snapshot algorithms
by
Jayanti 2005
. A snapshot algorithm scans a memory array
and returns the values read, so that the obtained values reflect the
state of the array at one point in time. In a sequential setting this
is trivial to achieve since the array remains unchanged during the
scan. However, Jayanti’s algorithms are concurrent, allowing
interfering threads to modify the array while a scan is in progress.
Jayanti’s algorithms are of increasing efficiency and generality. The
simplest is the single-writer/single-scanner algorithm, which assumes
that no two scanners run concurrently, and that no two writers
concurrently modify the same array element. Jayanti’s second
algorithm generalizes to a multi-writer/single-scanner setting, and
the third is the most general and ultimately desirable
multi-writer/multi-scanner version.
Jayanti describes the linearization points only for the
single-writer/single-scanner algorithm, but already this description
is quite challenging to transform into a fully formal correctness
proof
(
Delbianco et al. 2017
)
because the algorithm exhibits far
future linearization points.

Of interest to us in the current paper is that each of the three
algorithms builds on the previous one by relaxing some part of the
previous algorithm’s implementation, while preserving the essential
invariants that Jayanti calls
forwarding principles
. Jayanti
credits the forwarding principles as the key idea behind his design,
because the principles are shared by the three algorithms, and
abstractly govern how a concurrent write into the array should be
“forwarded” to a scanner that is in progress, but has already read
past the written element.

In this paper, we show how the forwarding principles can be
axiomatized mathematically in terms of visibility, which we present in

Section

2
(Jayanti states the principles in English, much
less formally). In
Section

3
, we develop the
linearizability proof out of the axioms alone, so that it applies to
all three algorithms simultaneously.
We then establish that the axioms hold for each of the three
algorithms: in
Section

3
for the first algorithm and

Section

4
for the second and third. In
Section

5
we
apply our method to verify another snapshot algorithm, that of

Afek et al. 1993
.

By employing visibility, we sidestep the difficulties inherent in
reasoning about linearization points in general, and far future
linearization points in particular.
Our development substantiates that visibility is a natural abstraction
to use in specifications and proofs, as it enables formally capturing
the intuition—that of forwarding principles—that motivated the
design of Jayanti’s algorithms in the first place.
In summary, our contributions are as follows.

•

This is the first formal proof of all of Jayanti’s three
snapshot algorithms. Moreover, it efficiently reuses proofs between
algorithms.
Delbianco et al. 2017
employed a
variation of the linearization point approach to prove only
Jayanti’s first algorithm, with no direct way of extending the proof
to the other two algorithms, which are significantly more involved.
That proof was mechanized in Coq. Based on
loc. cit.
,

Jacobs 2018
developed a mechanized proof of Jayanti’s first
algorithm in VeriFast, using prophecy variables.

•

We have axiomatized the forwarding principles, which were
Jayanti’s motivating insight, and key properties of his snapshot
algorithms, but have so far been out of reach of formal
mathematics. The axiomatization is non-trivial, and required
generalizing from Jayanti’s English description in order to apply to
all three algorithms. It also enabled us to prove, for the first
time, that forwarding principles formally imply linearizability.
While visibility relations have been used before to axiomatize
concurrent structures, this shows that they can also usefully
capture important
internal
properties.

2.
Overview

2.1.
Jayanti’s First Snapshot Algorithm

The
n
-snapshot data-structure is an array of length
n
, which
consists of two kinds of operations:
scan
which reads the
memory and returns a list of length
n
reflecting the state of the
memory at a point in time; and
\textsc{write}(i,v)
which writes the
value
v
into memory cell
i
.
We use
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
to range over instances of
scan
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}

to range over instances of
\textsc{write}(i,v)
for some
v
, and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}
to range over all operations in general. In line with
standard terminology of linearizability, we call these operations

abstract events
or
abs events
and color them

magenta
.

A snapshot (or any other) algorithm is linearizable if for every
concurrent execution, there exists some way of sequentially ordering
the
overlapping
(abs) events so that the value returned by each
of the events remains unchanged compared to the concurrent execution,
and moreover, matches the intended semantics of the data structure.
Intuitively, linearizability implies that we can re-run the
computation sequentially to obtain the same results as in the original
concurrent run; however, the internal state of the algorithm during
and after the runs need not match.

Implementing an efficient and correct concurrent snapshot algorithm is
more challenging than it may seem. To highlight this point, consider a
naïve implementation, where writers simply write to a shared array

\mathtt{A}
and scanners simply iterate over the array
\mathtt{A}
to obtain a
snapshot. To allow this implementation to operate efficiently, we
allow writes and scans to run concurrently. For this simple
implementation, consider a snapshot array of length
2
, where we have a
scan
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
running concurrently with writes
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{0}}
writing

2
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}}
writing
3
. Let
0
be the initial value of each
array cell, and consider the following execution:

•

Scan
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
starts and reads
0
from
\mathtt{A}[0]
, after which
the scheduler interrupts
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
.

•

Write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{0}}
starts, writing
2
to
\mathtt{A}[0]
, and after

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{0}}
finishes, write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}}
writes
3
to
\mathtt{A}[1]
.

•

Scan
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
resumes, reading
3
from
\mathtt{A}[1]
. It then
returns the snapshot
(0,3)
.

The snapshot
(0,3)
should indicate that there exists a point in time
when the array consisted of that pair, however that is not the case.
The array started as
(0,0)
, followed by
(2,0)
after write

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{0}}
, and
(2,3)
after write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}}
, but none of these
states are reflected in the result. In a sense, the scan
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}

missed the write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{0}}
, yet it caught the write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}}
,
which occurred after
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{0}}
. Jayanti’s snapshot algorithms ensure
that writes are not missed by the scanner, as we explain next.

1:

\mathtt{A}:\mathbf{array}[n]\ \mathbf{of}\ \mathsf{val}

2:

\mathtt{B}:\mathbf{array}[n]\ \mathbf{of}\ \mathsf{val}\cup\left\{\bot\right\}

3:

\mathtt{X}:\mathbb{B}\vcentcolon=\mathtt{false}

4:

5:

write
(
i:\mathbb{N},v:\mathsf{val}
)
\triangleq

\triangleright

w_{i}

6:

\mathtt{A}[i]\vcentcolon=v

\triangleright

{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}

7:

x\leftarrow\mathtt{X}

\triangleright

{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{x}

8:

if

x

then

\mathtt{B}[i]\vcentcolon=v

\triangleright

{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}

9:

scan
:

\mathbf{array}[n]\ \mathbf{of}\ \mathsf{val}

\triangleq

\triangleright

s

10:

\mathtt{X}\vcentcolon=\mathtt{true}

\triangleright

{s}_{\mathit{on}}

11:

for

i\in\left\{0\dots n-1\right\}

do

12:

\mathtt{B}[i]\vcentcolon=\bot

\triangleright

{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}

13:

for

i\in\left\{0\dots n-1\right\}

do

14:

a\leftarrow\mathtt{A}[i]

\triangleright

{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}

15:

V[i]\vcentcolon=a

16:

\mathtt{X}\vcentcolon=\mathtt{false}

\triangleright

{s}_{\mathit{off}}

17:

for

i\in\left\{0\dots n-1\right\}

do

18:

b\leftarrow\mathtt{B}[i]

\triangleright

{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}

19:

if

b\neq\bot

then

V[i]\vcentcolon=b

20:

return

V

Algorithm 1:
Jayanti’s single-writer, single-scanner
snapshot algorithm over a memory of length
n
. Mnemonics on the
right identify the corresponding commands.

Algorithm

1
is the first and simplest of Jayanti’s snapshot
algorithms. The idea is for a scan to make two passes over the memory,
first over the main array
\mathtt{A}
, and then over the auxiliary array

\mathtt{B}
. A writer updates the array
\mathtt{B}
if it detects a concurrent
scan via the boolean flag
\mathtt{X}
, to
forward
its value. That
is, in case the scanner missed the writer’s value when scanning

\mathtt{A}
, it will have a chance to catch the value when scanning

\mathtt{B}
. We thus refer to
\mathtt{B}
as the
forwarding array
.

Algorithm

1
is a single-writer/single-scanner algorithm, meaning
that for it to behave correctly, two scans must not run concurrently
and two writers must not concurrently mutate the same array cell. In
an implementation, this can be enforced by explicit locking, which we
elide from
Algorithm

1
following Jayanti’s original presentation.

Describing the algorithm in more detail,
scan
works by first
setting
\mathtt{X}
to
\mathtt{true}
by event
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{on}}}
, signaling that a
scan is running the first pass. This is followed by clearing the
forwarding array
\mathtt{B}
by setting all its cells to
\bot
via the

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}
events, for each
i
. The clearing ensures that the
current scan cannot consider the forwards left over from the previous
scans.
Next, the scanner creates a naïve snapshot by copying the main array

\mathtt{A}
by the events
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}
for each
i
into the local
array
V
. However, as we argued before, this is insufficient for a
correct snapshot. This is where the second pass comes in, which starts
after
\mathtt{X}
is set to
\mathtt{false}
by event
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{off}}}
.
In the second pass, the procedure repairs the naïve snapshot by
stepping through the forwarding array
\mathtt{B}
by the events

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
for each
i
. If a non-
\bot
(i.e., forwarded)
value is found, it overwrites the original value in
V
, thus
repairing the snapshot and preventing missing writes. Finally, the
scanner returns
V
, which contains the complete snapshot.

For
\textsc{write}(i,v)
, it starts by writing its value to
\mathtt{A}
by
event
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
, followed by a check for a concurrently
running scanner performing the naïve pass of the scan by event

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{x}}
. If such a scan is detected, the writer forwards its
value to
\mathtt{B}
by event
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}}
.

Events marked in
blue
are called

representation
or
rep
events, and are used internally in
the implementation of abs (i.e.,
magenta
) events. The
distinction between abs and rep events is standard in the theory of
linearizability
(
Herlihy and Wing 1990
)
.
We will use the naming convention whereby abs events and rep events
with the same priming belong together, e.g. we assume

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}
belongs to
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
belongs
to
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}}
.

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{on}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{0}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{1}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{0}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{1}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{off}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{0}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{1}}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{0}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{0}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{0}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{x}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{0}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{0}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{0}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}}
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{1}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}

Figure 1
.
Diagram illustrating an execution scenario for

Algorithm

1
with an array length of two.

Now, consider the scenario illustrated in
Fig.

1
,
where we have
Algorithm

1
being executed over an array of length
two, with each value initially set to
0
, with write events

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{0}}
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{0}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}}
writing 2, 3 and 4
respectively, and a scan
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
. When
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
reads
\mathtt{A}[0]

with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{0}}}
for its first pass, it reads 0, while when it
reads
\mathtt{A}[1]
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{1}}}
it reads 4 written by

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{1}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
.
Between the two passes of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{0}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
is missed,
however since
\mathtt{X}
is set to
\mathtt{true}
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{on}}}
,

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{0}}
will forward the value 2 with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{0}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}}
.
At the second pass,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
reads
\mathtt{B}[0]
with

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{0}}}
, reading 2 written by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{0}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}}
, and when
it reads
\mathtt{B}[1]
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{1}}}
, it reads
\bot
written
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{1}}}
, meaning
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
will use the original value 4
for its final snapshot, thus returning (2,4).

In contrast to
(0,3)
before, linearizability admits
(2,4)
as a
correct snapshot even though
\mathtt{A}
never contained
(2,4)
during
the execution. Indeed,
\mathtt{A}
only contained
(0,0)
,
(2,0)
,

(3,0)
and
(3,4)
. This is actually fine, because we can reorder the
concurrent events with the order

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{0}}\to{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}}\to{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}\to{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{0}}
, which, when
executed sequentially, result in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
having snapshot
(2,4)
.
In the physical execution, the event
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{0}}
returned before

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{0}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}}
started. The reordering respects this by
listing
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{0}}
before
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{0}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}}
. In other
words, the reordering affects only events that physically overlapped,
as required by linearizability.

Jayanti sketches the correctness proof of
Algorithm

1
by
describing its linearization points.
The linearization point for a scan
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
is always when the scan
performs
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{off}}}
. However, the linearization point of a write

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
varies. If there is no scan concurrent to
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
, or
there is a concurrent scan, but it reads the value of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}

either from
\mathtt{A}
or from
\mathtt{B}
, then the writer’s linearization
point is at
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
. If there is a concurrent scan
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}

that misses
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
, which can occur if the scanner misses

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
in its
\mathtt{A}
pass, and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
either does not write
into
\mathtt{B}[i]
due to
\mathtt{X}
being set to
\mathtt{false}
before the writer
could forward, or
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
writing into
\mathtt{B}[i]
too late,
then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
must be logically considered as occurring after

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
. Thus, the linearization point of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
is immediately
after
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{off}}}
, making the linearization point of

write

external
, as its position is given in terms of
another procedure, in this case
scan
.
The observation that a scan missed a write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
, which occurs
when the scan reads
\mathtt{B}[i]
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
, can be made
after the writer has already terminated, making
write
exhibit a
far future linearization point.

We proceed to show how to organize the linearizability proof
of
Algorithm

1
, and the other two Jayanti algorithms, by
axiomatizing forwarding via visibility, without using linearization
points.

2.2.
Basic Abstractions of Visibility Reasoning

2.2.1.
Events and Their Structure

An event is an object consisting of fields
\mathsf{start}
,
\mathsf{end}
,

\mathsf{op}
,
\mathsf{in}
, and
\mathsf{out}
, describing the following aspects of
the execution of some operation of the data structure:
\mathsf{start}
is
the event’s beginning time,
\mathsf{end}
is the ending time,
\mathsf{op}
is
the operation name (e.g.,
scan
or
write
),
\mathsf{in}
is
the operation’s input, and
\mathsf{out}
is the output. We refer to the
elements of an event
e
by projection, e.g.
e.\mathsf{start}
and

e.\mathsf{op}
.
The fields
e.\mathsf{start}
and
e.\mathsf{end}
are natural numbers, and

e.\mathsf{end}
may be
\infty
(infinity) to represent that
e
has not
terminated yet, i.e.,
e
is terminated iff
e.\mathsf{end}\neq\infty
,
which we denote with
\mathcal{T}(e)
.
For every
e
,
e.\mathsf{end}>e.\mathsf{start}
. The types of
e.\mathsf{in}
and

e.\mathsf{out}
depend on
e.\mathsf{op}
, and
e.\mathsf{out}
is undefined iff

e.\mathsf{end}=\infty
.

Additionally, each event
e
contains the optional field
\mathsf{parent}
,
corresponding to the event that invoked
e
, if any. For example, if

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e}
is a
rep
event, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e}.\mathsf{parent}
is the

abs
event that contains
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e}
. Note that
e.\mathsf{parent}
and

e
need not have the same
\mathsf{op}
,
\mathsf{in}
and
\mathsf{out}
fields; e.g.,
a write
rep
event can be invoked both by
abs
writer and

abs
scanner.

Finally, we require that each
abs
event is single-threaded, and
thus cannot fork children threads.

We denote the set of all events of a given execution history by

\mathsf{Events}
.
If
E\subset\mathsf{Events}
, then

\mathit{op}(E)=\left\{e\in E\mid e.\mathsf{op}=op\right\}
selects
the events with operation
\mathit{op}
, and we overload
\mathcal{T}
over
sets with
\mathcal{T}(E)=\left\{e\in E\mid\mathcal{T}(e)\right\}
to
select the terminated events.

As linearization order can only affect physically overlapping events,
proving linearizability requires reasoning about non-overlapping
events, which is captured by the
returns-before
relation

e\sqsubset e^{\prime}\mathrlap{\quad\triangleq}\hphantom{{}\implies{}}e.\mathsf{end}<e^{\prime}.\mathsf{start}

denoting that
e
terminated before
e^{\prime}
started. Two events are
overlapping if they are unrelated by
\sqsubset
. As customary, we write

\sqsubseteq
for the reflexive closure of
\sqsubset
.
The relation
\sqsubset
is irreflexive (i.e.
acyclic
) partial order,
and moreover, an
interval
order
(
Felsner 1992
)
, as it
satisfies the following property

(
RB.1
)

e_{1}\sqsubset e_{2}\land e^{\prime}_{1}\sqsubset e^{\prime}_{2}\implies e_{1}\sqsubset e^{\prime}_{2}\lor e^{\prime}_{1}\sqsubset e_{2}

\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{start}
e_{1}
\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{end}
\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{start}
e_{2}
\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{end}
\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{start}
e^{\prime}_{1}
\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{end}
\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{start}
e^{\prime}_{2}
\scriptscriptstyle\mathsf{end}

Figure 2
.
Visual representation of events highlighting the interval
and subevent
Eqs.

RB.1
and
RB.2
.

Fig.

2
illustrates why
Eq.

RB.1
must hold. The
figure shows events
e_{1},e_{2},e^{\prime}_{1},e^{\prime}_{2}
such that
e_{1}\sqsubset e_{2}
,
and
e^{\prime}_{1}\sqsubset e^{\prime}_{2}
, and
Eq.

RB.1
holds because also

e_{1}\sqsubset e^{\prime}_{2}
. We can try to invalidate the latter by shifting
e^{\prime}_{1}

and
e^{\prime}_{2}
to the left so that
e^{\prime}_{2}.\mathsf{start}<e_{1}.\mathsf{end}
while
maintaining
e^{\prime}_{1}.\mathsf{end}<e^{\prime}_{2}.\mathsf{start}
. But then we’re forced to
have
e^{\prime}_{1}.\mathsf{end}<e_{2}.\mathsf{start}
, i.e.
e^{\prime}_{1}\sqsubset e_{2}
which
re-establishes
Eq.

RB.1
.

We also say that
e
is a
subevent
of
e^{\prime}
(alternatively,
e^{\prime}

contains

e
) if

e\subseteq e^{\prime}\mathrlap{\quad\triangleq}\hphantom{{}\implies{}}e^{\prime}.\mathsf{start}\leq e.\mathsf{start}\land e.\mathsf{end}\leq e^{\prime}.\mathsf{end}

For any
rep
event
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e}
, we require that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e}.\mathsf{parent}

must be an
abs
event such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e}.\mathsf{parent}
.
Additionally, the following property holds for
subevents and returns-before.

(
RB.2
)

e_{1}\subseteq e^{\prime}_{1}\land e_{2}\subseteq e^{\prime}_{2}\land e^{\prime}_{1}\sqsubset e^{\prime}_{2}\implies e_{1}\sqsubset e_{2}

That is, if
e^{\prime}_{1}
returned before
e^{\prime}_{2}
then all subevents of
e^{\prime}_{1}

must return before any subevent of
e^{\prime}_{2}
. For example, in

Fig.

2
,
e_{1}\subseteq e^{\prime}_{1}
and
e^{\prime}_{2}\subseteq e_{2}
, and

e_{1}\sqsubset e^{\prime}_{2}
. If we shift
e^{\prime}_{1}
to the left so that
e^{\prime}_{1}\sqsubset e_{2}
,
then we just increase the distance between
e_{1}
and
e^{\prime}_{2}
,
maintaining
e_{1}\sqsubset e^{\prime}_{2}
.

2.2.2.
Visibility Relations

If the event
e^{\prime}
depends on the result of
e
, we say that
e
is

visible
to
e^{\prime}
, or alternatively that
e
is
observed
by

e^{\prime}
. We denote the relationship as

e\prec e^{\prime}

Depending on the data structure being verified, we will often require
several different observation relations to differentiate how the
observation came about. For example, in the case of Jayanti, we will
use
\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}
for a “reads-from observation” (a reader observes a
writer by reading what was written), and
\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}
for a “forwarding
observation” (a scanner observes a writer by having the written value
forwarded).
The former relation is tied to the physical act of reading a pointer,
and will be the same in all three algorithms. The latter relation
differs for different algorithms and is typically provided by the
human verifier, similarly to how loop invariants must often be
provided.

We will typically obtain the visibility relation
\prec
by unioning
all the different observation subrelations. We then require the
following property of
\prec

(
V.1
)

\displaystyle e\prec^{+}e^{\prime}\implies e^{\prime}\not\sqsubseteq e

where
\prec^{+}
is the transitive closure of
\prec
. Read
contrapositively, the property says that if
e^{\prime}
terminated before
e

started, then
e^{\prime}
cannot end a non-empty sequence of observations
starting from
e
. In particular, as a special case,
e^{\prime}
cannot
observe
e
or be equal to
e
, meaning
\prec
has to be irreflexive.
We write
\preccurlyeq
and
\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xRightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}
for the reflexive closure of
\prec

and
\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}
respectively.

2.2.3.
Happens-Before Relation

Given two events
e
and
e^{\prime}
, if
e\sqsubset e^{\prime}
or
e\prec e^{\prime}
, then
clearly, in the ultimate linearization order we want to construct,
e

must appear before
e^{\prime}
. To capture this intuition, we define
the
happens-before
relation as the transitive closure

\mathord{\vartriangleleft}\mathrlap{\quad\triangleq}\hphantom{{}\implies{}}(\mathord{\sqsubset}\ \cup\mathord{\prec})^{+}

We also name the
single-step happens-before
relation

\mathord{\vartriangleleft_{1}}\,{=}\,(\mathord{\sqsubset}\ \cup\ \mathord{\prec})
, so that

\mathord{\vartriangleleft}=\mathord{\vartriangleleft_{1}\trianglelefteqslant}\ =\ \mathord{\trianglelefteqslant\vartriangleleft_{1}}=\mathord{\vartriangleleft_{1}^{+}}
, where
\mathord{\trianglelefteqslant}
is the
reflexive-transitive closure
\mathord{\vartriangleleft_{1}^{*}}
.
The
\sqsubset
,
\prec
and
\vartriangleleft
relations are all standard in the
literature
(
Viotti and
Vukolić 2016
)
.
An important property is that
\vartriangleleft
is an irreflexive (i.e.,

acyclic
) partial order, which is ensured by
\prec

satisfying
Eq.

V.1
.

Lemma 2.1.

If visibility relation
\prec
satisfies
Eq.

V.1
then
\vartriangleleft

is irreflexive.

Proof.

We assume that
\vartriangleleft
is not irreflexive (i.e., there exists
e
such
that
e\vartriangleleft e
), and derive contradiction. The relation
e\vartriangleleft e
is
a cyclic chain of
\vartriangleleft_{1}
, each of which is either
\sqsubset
or

\prec
. We are justified in considering chains with only one or zero
occurrences of
\sqsubset
, as chains with more occurrences of
\sqsubset
can
be shortened, thus we can recursively shorten a chain until it
consists of one or zero
\sqsubset
. Indeed, if the chain has
more than one
\sqsubset
, it has the form

e\vartriangleleft_{1}\cdots\vartriangleleft_{1}e_{1}\sqsubset e_{2}\prec\cdots\prec e_{3}\sqsubset e_{4}\vartriangleleft_{1}\cdots\vartriangleleft_{1}e\ ,

where
e_{2}
and
e_{3}
are related by a chain of zero or more

\prec
’s (i.e.,
e_{2}=e_{3}
or
e_{2}\prec^{+}e_{3}
). But then, we can
remove one
\sqsubset
as follows. If
e_{2}=e_{3}
, by transitivity of

\sqsubset
, we can shorten the chain to
e\trianglelefteqslant e_{1}\sqsubset e_{4}\trianglelefteqslant e
. If

e_{2}\prec^{+}e_{3}
, by
Eq.

RB.1
, it must be either

e_{1}\sqsubset e_{4}
or
e_{3}\sqsubset e_{2}
. If
e_{1}\sqsubset e_{4}
, we again shorten
to
e\trianglelefteqslant e_{1}\sqsubset e_{4}\trianglelefteqslant e
. Otherwise,
e_{3}\sqsubset e_{2}
and

e_{2}\prec^{+}e_{3}
contradict
Eq.

V.1
.

On the other hand, if there is exactly one
\sqsubset
in the chain, i.e.,

e\prec^{*}e_{1}\sqsubset e_{2}\prec^{*}e
, then
e_{2}\prec^{*}e\prec^{*}e_{1}
,
i.e.,
e_{2}\prec^{*}e_{1}
which, along with
e_{1}\sqsubset e_{2}
, contradicts

Eq.

V.1
. Finally, if there is no
\sqsubset
in the chain, i.e.,

e\prec^{+}e
, then
Eq.

V.1
directly implies the
contradiction
e\not\sqsubseteq e
.
∎

We will usually color the relations with the same colors as the events
they are relating (e.g.,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e\prec e^{\prime}}
for abs events
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e_{r}\vartriangleleft e^{\prime}_{r}}
for rep events
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e_{r}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e^{\prime}_{r}}
).

2.2.4.
Memory Model

signature

\mathsf{AReg}

\triangleq

Atomic Register

Let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}W}

Set of all writes of the register of the history

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}R}

Set of all reads of the register of the history

\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sum$}}}{\hskip 15.00002pt}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\prec}

{}\subseteq({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}W}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}R})^{2}

Visibility relation

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}

{}\subseteq\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\prec}}\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}W}\times{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}R}

Reads-from visibility

Let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\vartriangleleft}

{}\mathrlap{\quad\triangleq}\hphantom{{}\implies{}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\sqsubset}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\prec})^{+}

Happens-before order

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e^{\prime}}.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e\prec^{+}e^{\prime}}

{}\implies{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e^{\prime}\not\sqsubseteq e}

(
V.1
)

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}r}\in\mathcal{T}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}R}).

\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}W}.\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}r}\land{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w}.\mathsf{in}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}r}.\mathsf{out}

(
M.1
)

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}W},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}r}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}R}.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}r}

{}\implies\nexists{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w^{\prime}}.\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}\vartriangleleft r}

(
M.2
)

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}W},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}r}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}R}.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}r}\land{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}r}

{}\implies{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w=w^{\prime}}

(
M.3
)

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}W}.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\neq w^{\prime}}

{}\implies{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}}\lor{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w^{\prime}\vartriangleleft w}

(
M.4
)

Figure 3
.
Signature representation of the properties of an atomic
register.

With the above relations we can now state as axioms the properties
that we expect of the underlying memory model. We start with a simple
axiomatization of memory that is sufficient for
Algorithm

1
. We
will extend this axiomatization in
Section

4
to account
for the
\mathbf{LL}
(load-link) and
\mathbf{SC}
(store-conditional) memory
operations, required by
Algorithms

2
and
3
.

The memory model only considers operations over individual memory
cells, aka. atomic registers
(
Herlihy and
Shavit 2008
)
. Their
axiomatization in terms of visibility relations is given in

Fig.

3
, in the form of a signature we assume the memory to
satisfy. We use
blue
in this figure, since we will only use
events of atomic registers as rep events.
The sets
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}W}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}R}
are defined by the history as the
sets of writes and reads respectively. The quantifier
\Sigma

signifies that the relations
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\prec}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}
are
abstract
components of the specification; the clients do not know anything
about these components outside of the listed axioms. Given an instance

X
of the signature, the clients can refer to the relations and to
the let definitions by projection as
X.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\prec}
,
X.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}
,

X.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}W}
and
X.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}R}
.
We will present similar signatures for snapshot data-structures and Jayanti-style forwarding.

Looking at
Fig.

3
,
an atomic register is mathematically represented by two sets of
events:
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}W}
, corresponding to all the writes into the register’s
memory cell, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}R}
, corresponding to all the reads. These are
related by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}r}
, stating that read
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}r}

reads
from
(i.e., observes) write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w}
. These events may also be
related by some other relation part of the total set of observations

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\prec}
, which includes
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}
;
Eq.

V.1
must hold
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\prec}
to ensure that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\vartriangleleft}
is irreflexive (thus, a
partial order) as per
Lemma

2.1
.

Fig.

3
states that for any terminated read
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}r}
there
exists an observed write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w}
with its input being the same as
the output of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}r}
.
Fig.

3
states that the
read
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}r}
can observe only the latest write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w}
into the
given memory cell.
Fig.

3
states that a read may
observe at most one write.
Fig.

3
states that the
writes into the given cell are totally ordered. These are standard
properties of sequentially consistent memory

(
Herlihy and
Shavit 2008
)
.

We want to reason about
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\vartriangleleft}
order arbitrarily between rep
events, even if they belong to distinct register objects. As an
analogue to the locality property of linearizability, which says that
the union of multiple linearizable objects is itself linearizable, we
establish that the union of objects satisfying
Eq.

V.1
itself
satisfies
Eq.

V.1
. By
Lemma

2.1
, this implies that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\vartriangleleft}
order between any rep event is a partial order.
More formally, let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}R_{1}}\dots{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}R_{m}}
be
all objects satisfying
\mathsf{AReg}
and representing our memory. We
define the top-level
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\prec}
(and top-level
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\vartriangleleft}
) relation
over rep events as the union of visibility of all register objects:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathord{\prec}}

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\quad\triangleq}\hphantom{{}\implies{}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}R_{1}.\mathord{\prec}})\ \cup\dots\cup\ ({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}R_{m}.\mathord{\prec}})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathord{\vartriangleleft}}

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\quad\triangleq}\hphantom{{}\implies{}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathord{\sqsubset}}\ \cup\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathord{\prec}})^{+}

Since each register satisfies
Eq.

V.1
and each individual

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\prec}
only relates events from the same register, it follows
that the combined
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\prec}
also satisfies
Eq.

V.1
. By

Lemma

2.1
, the top-level
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\vartriangleleft}
is then a partial
order. The combination similarly satisfies

Figs.

3
,
3
,
3
and
3
.
We will use these top-level definition when relating
rep events of distinct objects.

2.2.5.
Linearizability

We now define linearizability formally in terms of visibility relations.

Definition 2.2.

History
E
is linearizable with respect to data structure

\mathcal{D}
if there exists a visibility relation
\prec
and a total order
<
on the set
of events

E_{c}=\{e\mid\exists e^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}(E).\ e\prec^{*}e^{\prime}\}
, such
that: (1)
\mathord{\vartriangleleft}=(\sqsubset\cup\prec)^{+}\subseteq\mathord{<}

on
E_{c}
, and (2) executing the events in
E_{c}
in the order of
<

is a legal sequential behavior of
\mathcal{D}
. The order
<
is
the
linearization order
(or
linearization
, for short)
of
E
. Algorithm (or structure)
\mathcal{A}
is linearizable
wrt.
\mathcal{D}
if every history of
\mathcal{A}
is linearizable
wrt.
\mathcal{D}
.

Definition
2.2
differs somewhat from the original
one
(
Herlihy and Wing 1990
)
in that we use the visibility relation
\prec
to
complete
\mathcal{T}(E)
into
E_{c}
with events that have been observed,
but have not yet terminated. The original definition existentially
abstracts over the set of completing events; formally, it does not
organize them into a visibility relation, but in practice these events
are always added because they have been observed by some terminated
event, and are necessary to ensure legal sequential behavior.
The requirement
\mathord{\vartriangleleft}\subseteq\mathord{<}
means that
<

must respect
\sqsubset
(in addition to
\prec
), and in particular that
<

can reorder only overlapping events.
In this paper,
\mathcal{D}
is the snapshot data structure that has
the following sequential behavior over its state (the array
\mathtt{A}
):

•

w_{i}\in\textsc{write}_{i}
writes
w_{i}.\mathsf{in}
to
\mathtt{A}[i]
and
returns nothing, i.e.,
w_{i}.\mathsf{out}=()
.

•

s\in\textsc{scan}
does not modify
\mathtt{A}
and returns a copy of

\mathtt{A}
, i.e.,
s.\mathsf{out}=\mathtt{A}
.

Hierarchical Structure of the Proof

Algorithm

1
\mathsf{Forwarding}
sig.
(
Fig.

7
)
\mathsf{Snapshot}
sig.
(
Fig.

5
)
Linearizability
(Def.
2.2
)
Algorithm

2
Algorithm

3
\mathsf{MWForwarding}
sig.
(
Fig.

11
)
Step (1)
Lemma

3.3
Step (2)
Lemma

3.2
Step (3)
Lemma

2.3
Step (1a)
Lemma

4.6
Step (1a)
Lemma

4.7
Step
(2a)
Lemma
4.5

Figure 4
.
Overview of the structure of the linearizability proof for each
of Jayanti’s snapshot algorithms.

Ultimately, our goal is to prove that each of Jayanti’s three snapshot
algorithms is linearizable. To accomplish this in a way that maximizes
proof reuse, we divide our proofs into multiple segments, which we
illustrate in
Fig.

4
. The proof of Jayanti’s first
algorithm is split into the following steps:

(1)

Jayanti’s single-writer/single-scanner algorithm
(
Algorithm

1
) consists of the rep events described in

Fig.

6
and satisfies the forwarding snapshot
signature that we give in
Fig.

7
.

(2)

Any algorithm consisting of the rep events described in

Fig.

6
and satisfying the forwarding snapshot signature
from
Fig.

7
, also satisfies the general snapshot
signature in
Fig.

5
.

(3)

Any algorithms satisfying the general snapshot signature in

Fig.

5
is linearizable.

For Jayanti’s remaining algorithms, we incorporate the following two
additional steps.

(1a)

Jayanti’s two multi-writer algorithms
(
Algorithms

2
and
3
) consist of the rep events described in

Fig.

10
and satisfy the multi-writer forwarding snapshot
signature in
Fig.

11
.

(2a)

Any algorithm consisting of the rep events described in

Fig.

10
and satisfying the multi-writer forwarding
snapshot signature in
Fig.

11
, also satisfies the
forwarding snapshot signature in
Fig.

7
. The
linearizability then follows by steps (2) and (3) above, which are
thus reused and shared by all three algorithms.

We proceed to describe the intuition behind the steps (1) to (3),
together with the forwarding and snapshot signatures. The detailed
proofs of (1) to (3) are in
Section

3
. The description
and proofs of (1a) and (2a) are in
Section

4
.

2.4.
Snapshot Signature

signature

\mathsf{Snapshot}

\triangleq

Happens-before order

\forall{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e^{\prime}}.

{}\implies{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e^{\prime}\not\sqsubseteq e}

)

\forall i,{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}\in\mathcal{T}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}S}).

\forall{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}.

{}\implies\nexists{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}}.\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}\vartriangleleft s}

(
S.2
)

\forall{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}.

{}\implies{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}}\lor{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\vartriangleleft w_{i}}

(
S.4
)

(

\forall{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s^{\prime}}.

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i},w_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}\land{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s^{\prime}}\land{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}}\implies{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j}\not\vartriangleleft w_{j}}

(
S.6
)

Figure 5
.
Signature representation of the properties of a
snapshot data-structure.

Going backwards, we start with the general snapshot signature
(
Fig.

5
). It describes the axioms for reasoning about the
snapshot data structure as a whole, and will interface the
linearizablity proofs by the following lemma, which we will prove in

Section

3
.

Lemma 2.3.

Histories satisfying the
\mathsf{Snapshot}
signature
(
Fig.

5
) are linearizable.

The
\mathsf{Snapshot}
signature is almost identical to that for atomic
registers (
Fig.

3
); the main distinction is that we have
multiple sets of writes into multiple pointers, scans (observing
multiple writes) instead of reads, the sets of
effectful
writes

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathbb{W}_{i}}
, and the extra
Figs.

5
and
5
.

Not all writes are immediately observable once they have started,
therefore we introduce the set of effectful writes
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathbb{W}_{i}}

for each
i
. These are the writes that are available for scans to
observe (by definition of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}
) and which we can order by

Fig.

5
.
Fig.

5
encodes that each terminated
write must have been effectful.

Fig.

5
imposes a form of monotonicity on the ordering of
the writes observed by scans over multiple memory cells. More
specifically, if we have two writes
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}}
observed
by scan
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
, and two writes
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}}
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j}}
observed
by scan
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s^{\prime}}
, then the writes into
i
and
j
cannot be ordered
in the opposite way.
The property ensures that the scans can be totally ordered in an
eventual linearization order. Indeed, if
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}}
and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j}\vartriangleleft w_{j}}
, and we ordered
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
before
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s^{\prime}}
, then
we reach a contradiction by
Fig.

5
, because the
event
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}}
occurs between
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j}}
and the scan
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s^{\prime}}

that observes it. Similar argument applies if we try to order the
scans the other way around.

2.5.
Forwarding Signature

The
\mathsf{Snapshot}
signature in
Fig.

5
captures the key
properties of snapshot algorithms, and we shall prove linearizability
solely out of the axioms of this signature. However, Jayanti’s
algorithms have more in common than merely being snapshot algorithms,
as they share the same design principle of forwarding. We can thus
further modularize the proofs by axiomatizing forwarding itself.

To see what the axiomatization should accomplish, we must first
discuss the high-level differences between Jayanti’s algorithms that
the axiomatization must abstract over. We present

Algorithms

2
and
3
in detail in
Section

4
,
but for now it suffices to know that in order to support the
multi-writer functionality of
Algorithms

2
and
3
, we need to
allow for different kinds of forwarding that go beyond simply writing
into the auxiliary array
\mathtt{B}
that
Algorithm

1
does.

Additionally, to support multi-scanner functionality of

Algorithm

3
, we need to allow for an abstract notion of

virtual
scan. While in a multi-scanner setting several physical
scans may run concurrently, the key idea of Jayanti for
Algorithm

3

is that these scans collaborate to create a virtual scan (and a
corresponding snapshot), of which at most one may exist at any given
moment. Thus, even though
Algorithm

3
is physically a
multi-scanner, in
Section

4.3
we will still be able to
conceptually see it as a single-scanner one.

While rep and abs events originate from execution histories, the
virtual scans are artificial events that the human verifier creates
themselves for purposes of verification. In analogy with the concept
of “ghost state” that is frequently used in verification of
concurrent programs, we can say that virtual scans are “ghost”
events.
We will further require a mapping that sends each abs scan to a
virtual scan to which the abs scan contributed. Thus, in a
multi-scanner algorithm, a number of abs scans may be mapped to the
same virtual scan. The mapping may be partial because some abs scan
may not have yet reached a point for which the virtual scan
representing it has been determined. For single-scanner algorithms,
this map is a (total) bijection that identifies virtual and abs scans.
In particular, the intuition about abs scans from
Algorithm

1
will
suffice to understand virtual scans in our axiomatization of forwarding
principles.
In the rest of the text, we use the color
green
to visually
mark the virtual scans.

2.5.1.
Event Signatures

resource

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{A}}\mathrlap{\ \ :}\hphantom{{}\subseteq{}}\mathbf{array}[n]\ \mathbf{of}\ \mathsf{AReg}

Main memory array

event signature

\textsc{write}_{i}

\triangleq

Write into cell
i

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}a}

{}\mathrlap{\ \ :}\hphantom{{}\subseteq{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{A}[i].W}

Rep event of writer writing value into
\mathtt{A}[i]

event signature

vscan

\triangleq

Virtual scan

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{B}}

{}\mathrlap{\ \ :}\hphantom{{}\subseteq{}}\mathbf{array}[n]\ \mathbf{of}\ \mathsf{AReg}

Forwarding array of this virtual scan

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}r_{i}}

{}\mathrlap{\ \ :}\hphantom{{}\subseteq{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{B}[i].W}

Rep event of scanner resetting
\mathtt{B}[i]
by writing
\bot

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}a_{i}}

{}\mathrlap{\ \ :}\hphantom{{}\subseteq{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{A}[i].R}

Rep event of scanner reading from
\mathtt{A}[i]

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}b_{i}}

{}\mathrlap{\ \ :}\hphantom{{}\subseteq{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{B}[i].R}

Rep event of scanner reading from
\mathtt{B}[i]

Figure 6
.
Resources and event signatures for abs writes and
virtual scans corresponding to
\mathsf{Forwarding}
signature.

For the
\mathsf{Forwarding}
signature, it is not enough to just consider
properties over the abs events, as for the
\mathsf{Snapshot}
signature,
but we also need to consider their rep events. For this reason, we
introduce a special kind of signature, called
event signature
,
to encode which rep events an abs or virtual event consists of. In
particular, for an
abs
event
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}
, we write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}\in\textsc{sig}
, if
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}
’s rep events are enumerated by the fields
of the signature
sig
. If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}\in\textsc{sig}
, where

sig
contains the field
a
, we write
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{ea}}

for the projection
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}.{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}a}
. Similarly, if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}e}
is a

virtual
event.
We shall further assume the following properties of structures
satisfying event signatures:

(ES.1)

Let
e
be an event in a signature with field
a
. If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}

is an
abs
event, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{ea}}.\mathsf{parent}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}
,
and thus also
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{ea}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}
, by our assumption
on event structure in Section
2.2.1
. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}e}

is a
virtual
event, we do not insist on

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{ea}}.\mathsf{parent}={\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}e}
. Virtual event
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}e}
is
a custom collection of rep events, whose parents remain the abs
events that invoked them, not the collection that is

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}e}
. Nevertheless, we still require
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{ea}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}e}
.

(ES.2)

Any event in an event signature instance is unique to the
instance, i.e., if
e,e^{\prime}\in\textsc{sig}
, and
sig

contains the field
a
, and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{ea}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{e^{\prime}a}}
, then
e=e^{\prime}
.

(ES.3)

Instances of event signatures may have some (or all) of their
subevents undefined, unless the instance is a terminated event, in
which case all its subevents must be defined. For example, if

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}
is non-terminated,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{ea}}
may be undefined.
This corresponds to ongoing abs events having only a subset of its
rep events executed so far.

We now present
Fig.

6
which consists of the resources and
event signatures necessary for the
\mathsf{Forwarding}
signature.
First, we have the array
\mathtt{A}
which corresponds to the main memory
that a forwarding snapshot algorithm operates over.
Algorithm

1

clearly has such an array.
Next, we have that every abs write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
has a unique rep event

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
signifying the physical act of writing into

\mathtt{A}
. This is clearly true of
Algorithm

1
, as we have seen.
For a virtual scan
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}
, the figure postulates an array

\mathtt{B}
, as well as three rep events
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
, corresponding to
resetting
\mathtt{B}[i]
, reading from
\mathtt{A}[i]
, and reading from

\mathtt{B}[i]
, respectively. In
Algorithm

1
, we have already
described these events under the names
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
, and similar events will
exist for
Algorithms

2
and
3
as well. The notation implies that
the array
\mathtt{B}
and the subevents are local fields of

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}
. We will make use of the locality of
\mathtt{B}
in the
multi-scanner case, where different virtual scans have different
forwarding arrays. In the case of single-scanner algorithms, the
virtual and abs scans coincide, and the forwarding array of each
virtual scan is instantiated with the global forwarding array.

2.5.2.
Forwarding Signature Formally

signature

\mathsf{Forwarding}

\triangleq

Snapshot data-structure with forwarding

Let

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}W_{i}}

Set of all writes of cell
i
of the snapshot
in history with each
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}\in\textsc{write}_{i}

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}S}

Set of all scans of the snapshot in history

\mathrlap{\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.2}{$\displaystyle\sum$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\textstyle\sum$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptstyle\sum$}}{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sum$}}}{\hskip 15.00002pt}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\Sigma}

Set of virtual scans with each
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}\in\textsc{vscan}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}[-]^{\bullet}}

{}\mathrlap{\ \ :}\hphantom{{}\subseteq{}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}S}\rightharpoonup{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\Sigma}

Partial mapping of abs scans into virtual scans

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}}

Let

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}

{}\mathrlap{\quad\triangleq}\hphantom{{}\implies{}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}\land{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}})\lor{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}

Writing visibility

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\prec}

Visibility relation

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}

{}\mathrlap{\quad\triangleq}\hphantom{{}\implies{}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\sqsubset}\cup{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\prec})^{+}

Happens-before order

\forall{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}\in\mathsf{dom}({\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}[-]^{\bullet}}).

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}

(
F.1
)

\forall i,{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}\in\mathcal{T}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}S}).

\exists{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}.\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}}\land{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}.\mathsf{in}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}.\mathsf{out}[i]

(
F.2
)

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e_{r}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{A}[i].W}.

\exists{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}.\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e_{r}}

(

a)

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e_{r}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}.\mathtt{B}[i].W}.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e_{r}}.\mathsf{in}=\bot

{}\iff\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma^{\prime}}.\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e_{r}}

(

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma},{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\Sigma}.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma\neq\sigma^{\prime}}

{}\ \,\implies{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma\sqsubset\sigma^{\prime}}\lor{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma^{\prime}\sqsubset\sigma}

(

a)

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\Sigma}.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}\sqsubset{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}\sqsubset{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}

(

\forall{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}.

{}\ \,\implies{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}=w^{\prime}_{i}}

(
