{}\ \,\implies\exists{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}.\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}

(
F.5
b)

\forall{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}.

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}

{}\ \,\implies{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}\land\neg({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}})

(
F.5
c)

\forall{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}\land{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\sqsubset\trianglelefteqslant\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}

{}\ \,\implies{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}

(
F.6
)

\forall{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}.

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}\land{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}

{}\ \,\implies{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\not\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}}

(
F.7
a)

\forall{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}.

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}\land{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\sqsubset\trianglelefteqslant\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}

{}\ \,\implies{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\not\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}}

(
F.7
b)

Figure 7
.
Signature representation of the properties of a snapshot
data-structure that uses forwarding.

Our axiomatization of forwarding principles is given in

Fig.

7
.
The signature, which we refer to as
\mathsf{Forwarding}
, declares
as inputs the sets of write events
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}W_{i}}
(for each
i
), and the
set of scan events
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}S}
. It then postulates the existence of a
set of virtual scans
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\Sigma}
, with a mapping
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}[-]^{\bullet}}
from
abs to virtual scans, and a relation
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}}
for visibility by
forwarding that captures abstractly when a virtual scan observes a
writer by forwarding. As before, these are abstract concepts, known to
satisfy only the properties listed in the scope of
\Sigma
.
We will shortly provide the intuition for the axioms
in
Fig.

7
, but first, let us enumerate how the
axiomatization abstracts from
Algorithm

1
, so that the same
signature applies to all three algorithms.

•

The axiomatization does not assume that we have rep write events
for forwarding (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}}
). Instead, it makes forwarding
abstract by tying it to the relation
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}}
, allowing for
multiple different forwarding methods.

•

The axiomatization links virtual scans to abs scans by the
function
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}[-]^{\bullet}}
, to support multiple scanners. Physical scans
can overlap in time, as long as the virtual scans do not.

•

The axiomatization does not assume any rep events operating with
memory cell
\mathtt{X}
, or for that matter, that such a cell even
exists. Since
\mathtt{X}
is solely used to communicate to writers if and
when to forward, we instead encode the conditions for forwarding in
the axioms. This is necessary because the three algorithms decide
differently on whether to forward or not.

2.5.3.
Intuition Behind the Forwarding Signature

Out of the forwarding visibility relation
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}}
, we define
two additional visibility relations over abstract and virtual
events:
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wr}}$}}}$}}}}
as shown in

Fig.

7
. These relations define what we will use as an
abstract notion of a virtual scan observing a writer directly or by
forwarding (
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}
), and a writer observing a prior write
(
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wr}}$}}}$}}}}
), respectively. In each algorithm, the
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}}

relation will be defined differently, but the definitions of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wr}}$}}}$}}}}
are constant in terms
of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}}
. We next explain each of them.

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}\mathrlap{\quad\triangleq}\hphantom{{}\implies{}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}\land{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}})\lor{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}

Read visibility (
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}
) captures that a virtual scan

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}
either reads the value of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
by forwarding
(disjunct
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
) or directly. The direct read
requires that the scanner finds the value in
\mathtt{A}[i]
(conjunct

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}wra_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}
) and that the scanner finds
\bot

for the forwarded value in
\mathtt{B}[i]
(conjunct

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
).

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wr}}$}}}$}}}w^{\prime}_{i}}\mathrlap{\quad\triangleq}\hphantom{{}\implies{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}

Write visibility (
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wr}}$}}}$}}}}
) orders the abs writers of a common
memory cell, and is defined by the ordering of underlying rep writers

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
. Ordering abs writers this way works because the rep
write is the unique point where the abs writer communicates its value
to the data structure. This holds for all three algorithms including
the multi-writer variants.

We next explain the forwarding properties. These are divided into
three groups, as shown in
Fig.

7
. The first group consists
of the structural properties related to writers and scanners.

\forall{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}\in\mathsf{dom}({\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}[-]^{\bullet}}).

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}

(
7
revisited)

\forall i,{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}\in\mathcal{T}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}S}).

\exists{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}.\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}}\land{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}.\mathsf{in}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}.\mathsf{out}[i]

(
7
revisited)

Fig.

7
states that each virtual scan must be inside
the interval of the abs scan it represents.
Fig.

7
states
that a terminated abs scan
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
must have a virtual scan
representative that, for each index
i
, observed some write

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
such that the value written by
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
is the one the
scan
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
returned for
i
.
Fig.

7
is similar to

Fig.

5
, and essentially says that the observing scan correctly
reads the array. On the other hand,
Fig.

7
is similar
to a property of linearization points, whereby a linearization point
must reside within the interval of the considered event. Here instead,
we have a whole virtual scan
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}
representing when

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
logically occurred.

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e_{r}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{A}[i].W}.

\exists{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}.\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e_{r}}

(
7
revisited)

Fig.

7
ensures that the only rep event that can write
into
\mathtt{A}[i]
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
of some abs write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
.
This means that only abs writes into cell
i
can have a rep event for
writing into
\mathtt{A}[i]
; scans and writes into a cell different from

i
cannot, since if they did, they would have to be equal to some

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
as per Property
(ES.2)
of event signatures.
Also, an abs write can write into
\mathtt{A}[i]
at most once, since any
other write must equal
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
.

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e_{r}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}.\mathtt{B}[i].W}.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e_{r}}.\mathsf{in}=\bot

{}\iff\exists{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma^{\prime}}.\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e_{r}}

(
7
revisited)

The easiest way to explain
Fig.

7
is to consider its
instantiation for
Algorithm

1
, where virtual scanners are abs
scanners, which, moreover, all share the same array
\mathtt{B}
.

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e_{r}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathtt{B}[i].W}.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e_{r}}.\mathsf{in}=\bot

{}\iff\exists{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\sigma^{\prime}}.\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e_{r}}

(
F.3b’
)

The simplified
Section

2.5.3
says that the only rep event
that can write
\bot
into
\mathtt{B}[i]
is
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}
of
some
abs
scan
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\sigma^{\prime}}
. Thus, no event except scanners can
write
\bot
into
\mathtt{B}[i]
, and the writing can be done at most
once. Additionally, the rep event
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}
can

only
write
\bot
into
\mathtt{B}[i]
.
Fig.

7

generalizes
Section

2.5.3
by allowing for virtual scanners,
each of which has their own
\mathtt{B}
array.

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma},{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\Sigma}.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma\neq\sigma^{\prime}}

{}\implies{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma\sqsubset\sigma^{\prime}}\lor{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma^{\prime}\sqsubset\sigma}

(
7
revisited)

Fig.

7
captures that virtual scans never overlap, and
are thus totally ordered. This property holds for single-scanner
algorithms by assumption, but must be proved in the multi-scanner
case.

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\Sigma}.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}\sqsubset{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}\sqsubset{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}

(
7
revisited)

Fig.

7
simply reflects that the rep events of initializing
the scan for element
i
, reading
\mathtt{A}[i]
, and then closing the
scanning for element
i
by reading
\mathtt{B}[i]
, are invoked
sequentially in the code of
scan
. This is readily visible
in
Algorithm

1
and remains true in
Algorithms

2
and
3
.

The second group are the structural properties of the forwarding
visibility.

\forall{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}.

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}\land{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}

{}\implies{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}=w^{\prime}_{i}}

(
7
revisited)

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}.

\mathcal{T}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}})\land\neg({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}})

{}\implies\exists{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}.\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}

(
7
revisited)

\forall{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}.

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}

{}\implies{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}\land\neg({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}})

(
7
revisited)

Fig.

7
captures that a scan can observe at most
one forwarded write for a given index
i
.

Fig.

7
says that a forwarding of some write of cell
i

will reach the virtual scan
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
did
not observe the writing of
\bot
in
\mathtt{B}[i]
by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}
. Contrapositively,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
holds if no write of cell
i
was
forwarded to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}
.

Fig.

7
states the dual implication direction of

Fig.

7
and that a forwarded write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
must have
written into
\mathtt{A}[i]
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
before the scanner

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}
read the forwarded value by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
. This
property captures that writers must first write their value directly
before attempting to forward.

The third group are the properties that capture the forwarding
principles of Jayanti. We have originally discovered these properties
by extracting the common patterns from our proofs of the snapshot
signature for the three algorithms, and only afterwards discovered
that they actually correspond quite closely to Jayanti’s forwarding
principles. To describe how our axioms capture the forwarding
principles, we state the principles below in English, verbatim as Jayanti does,
but using our notation. We also use virtual scans instead of abstract
scans to make the connection to multi-scanner algorithm direct;
Jayanti only stated the principles in terms of
Algorithm

1
. As we
shall see, our axiomatization modifies the principles slightly to
encompass
Algorithms

2
and
3
.
We also highlight and number subsentences so that we can relate them
to our axioms in discussion.

(1)

Suppose that
(i)
a scan operation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}
misses a
write operation
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
writing
v
because
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}
reads

\mathtt{A}[i]
before
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
writes in
\mathtt{A}[i]
. If

(ii)
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
completes before
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}
performs

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{off}}}
, then
(iii)
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
will have surely
informed
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}
of
v
by writing
v
in
\mathtt{B}[i]
.

(2)

Suppose that
(iv)
a scan operation
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}
reads at

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
a non-
\bot
value in
\mathtt{B}[i]
by some
write operation
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
. Then (a)
(v)
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
is
concurrent with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}
, and (b) If
(vi)
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}}

is any write operation that is executed after
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
, then

(vii)
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}}
completes only after
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}
performs

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{off}}}
.

We start with
Fig.

7
, which relates to Principle (1) as
follows.

\forall{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}\land{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\sqsubset\trianglelefteqslant\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}

{}\implies{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}

(
7
revisited)

Here,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
corresponds to the
negation of Statement (iii),
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\sqsubset\trianglelefteqslant\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}

corresponds to Statement (ii), and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}
corresponds to the negation of Statement (i),
altogether combining into an equivalent, by contraposition, of
Principle (1). We now explain each correspondence individually.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
states that nothing
was forwarded to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}
,
because the event
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
of reading
\mathtt{B}[i]

observes the event
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}
of clearing
\mathtt{B}[i]
.
This is a slightly stronger statement than the negation of
Statement (iii), namely, that the write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
was not
forwarded to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}
. The difference between these statements
will be covered by
Fig.

7
, which will handle the
scenario where another write, not
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
, is forwarded to

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\sqsubset\trianglelefteqslant\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
, where

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\sqsubset\trianglelefteqslant\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}
is the relational composition of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\sqsubset}

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\trianglelefteqslant}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}
, roughly translates to
“
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
terminates before some other writer (possibly writing
to a different memory cell) that in turn was observed by

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}
”. We know such other writer exists, since the domain
of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}
, the last relation in the composition, consists
only of writers.
While this looks nothing like Statement (ii), both statements
imply that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
must have been forwarded if the writing to

\mathtt{A}[i]
was missed.
We prove this with different proofs for each algorithm, with
Lemma
C.2
for
Algorithm

1
and
Lemma
D.2
for
Algorithms

2
and
3
.
We use the statement
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\sqsubset\trianglelefteqslant\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
, instead
of potentially another statement involving
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{off}}}
(as
Jayanti’s original statement does), because it makes the forwarding
signature export fewer rep events, thus making it a bit more
parsimonious.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}
states that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
wrote to
\mathtt{A}[i]
before
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}

read from it, which directly corresponds to the negation of
Statement (i).

For Principle (2a), we need to further deviate from Jayanti’s original
formulation, since the latter is too specific to
Algorithm

1
, and
does not scale as-is to the multi-writer algorithms. The point of this
principle is to ensure that a writer may only forward values that are,
intuitively, “current”.
The principle itself ensures this property, but only in the
single-writer case of
Algorithm

1
. Indeed, in
Algorithm

1
, a
writer only forwards its own value (and other writes to the same
pointer are prohibited by assumption); therefore, if a write is
concurrent to the scan, then it can only forward a value that is
current. But in a multi-writer case, as we shall see
in
Section

4
, we want to allow a writer to forward values
of other writers. In particular, a writer that writes and then stalls
indefinitely could still be forwarded by another write to some
concurrent scan. But we still need to ensure that the forwarded write
is not arbitrarily old. We do so by insisting that a write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}

forwarded to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}
cannot occur before a write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}}

which wrote to
\mathtt{A}[i]
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
before
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}

started, i.e., executed
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}
. In other words, we
require that the forwarded write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
does not “happen
before” any write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}}
that started before the start of the
scan. A write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}}
has a
newer
value, so its existence
should prevent
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
from being forwarded. We formalize this as

Fig.

7
.

\forall{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}.

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}\land{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}

{}\implies{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\not\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}}

(
7
revisited)

This captures that writers must forward to scan
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}
either
the latest write that wrote to
\mathtt{A}[i]
before

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}
, or some write that occurred after

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}
, thus ensuring that the forwarded write is
current.

Lastly,
Fig.

7
relates to the contrapositive of
Principle (2b).

\forall{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}.

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}\land{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\sqsubset\trianglelefteqslant\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}

{}\implies{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\not\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}}

(
7
revisited)

Again,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
directly corresponds to
Statement (iv). Formal statement
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\sqsubset\trianglelefteqslant\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
corresponds to the negation of Statement (vii), for
the same reason as in
Fig.

7
, since (ii) is the
negation of (vii). Finally,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\not\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}}
directly
corresponds to the negation of (vi), with the exception that we
use
\vartriangleleft
instead of Jayanti’s “executed after” to relate the writes

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}}
. The two correspond in the single-writer
case, but our statement extends to the multi-writer case as well.

Algorithm

1
satisfies this set of properties of the
\mathsf{Forwarding}

signature, and they are sufficient to prove that an algorithm is a
snapshot algorithm according to the
\mathsf{Snapshot}
signature, which is
in turn sufficient to prove linearizability. In

Section

3
we sketch why these claims holds, and in

Section

4
we sketch how
Algorithms

2
and
3
satisfy
the
\mathsf{Forwarding}
signature.
The complete proofs are available in the appendix.

3.
Proof Sketches for Algorithm
1

We now sketch the proofs of our signatures implying linearizability,
to illustrate the gist of reasoning by visibility.
We begin by showing how histories that satisfy the
\mathsf{Snapshot}

signature are linearizable. This proof is based on a similar proof by

Chakraborty et al. 2015
for queues.

Lemma

2.3
0.

Histories satisfying
\mathsf{Snapshot}
signature
(
Fig.

5
) are linearizable.

Proof sketch.

Following
Definition

2.2
of linearizability, we start by choosing a
visibility relation
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\prec}
that constructs the set
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E_{c}}=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}\mid\exists{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{T}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E}).\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e\prec^{*}e^{\prime}}\}
, and then extend it to a linearization

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<}
. Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\prec}
be the visibility relation obtained by
restricting the visibility relation postulated by
\mathsf{Snapshot}
to

\mathcal{T}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}S})\cup\bigcup_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathbb{W}_{i}}
. Events outside of
the restriction are non-terminated scans or writes that have not
executed their effect yet; they do not modify the abstract state,
and are hence not necessary for linearization.
Additionally, by
Fig.

5
we know that every terminated
write is effectful. Thus going forward, we consider
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E_{c}}=\mathcal{T}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}S})\cup\bigcup_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathbb{W}_{i}}
.

We next extend the happens-before order
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}
on the selected
set of events as follows. The idea is to keep extending this order
with more relations between the events of the selected set until we
reach a total order, which will be the desired linearization order.
We first induce a helper order

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft_{w}}\mathrlap{\quad\triangleq}\hphantom{{}\implies{}}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}\ \cup\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wrDiff}}$}}}$}}}})^{+}

which ranges over writes (into different cells), where

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wrDiff}}$}}}$}}}w^{\prime}_{j}}
holds if
i\neq j
and there exists a write

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}}
and a scan
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i},w_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{j}}
. Or, in English, we order
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}

before
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j}}
, if
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
is observed by some scan along
with
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}}
is ordered before
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j}}
.
The
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft_{w}}
order is a (strict) partial order by

Figs.

5
,
5
,
5
,
5
and
5

(see Lemma
A.2

for the complete proof).

Next, we select an arbitrary total order
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
over writes
that extends
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft_{w}}
. Since the set of selected events is
finite, such a total order always exists by Zorn’s Lemma. We use the
order
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
to determine which event we will consider as
being last in the eventually constructed linearization order. We say
that a write is latest-in-time if it is the greatest in

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
, while a scan is latest-in-time if it is maximal in

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}
and all the writes that it observes are the greatest in

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
for their respective memory cell. As long as the set of
events is non-empty, there will exist a latest-in-time event under
this definition by

Figs.

5
,
5
,
5
and
5
(see
Lemma
A.4
for
the complete proof). By inductively selecting the latest-in-time
event, we construct a total order of events that is consistent with

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}
and snapshot semantics, and is thus a linearization
order. More concretely, we add the latest-in-time event to the end
of the linearization order, forming a chain with previously added
events, and repeat this step with the set of events excluding the
last selected.
∎

Next, we show that histories satisfying the
\mathsf{Forwarding}
signature also
satisfy the
\mathsf{Snapshot}
signature.
Because some
\mathsf{Forwarding}
properties use rep events, to be able to
efficiently use them, we first need a lemma that turns a
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}

relation between virtual or abs events into a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\vartriangleleft}
relation
between rep events. The lemma will also be useful in showing that

Algorithm

1
(and
Algorithms

2
and
3
in

Section

4
) satisfy the
\mathsf{Forwarding}
signature, once

Figs.

7
and
7
have been proven.

Lemma 3.1 (Happens-before of subevents).

Assume
Figs.

7
and
7
. For events

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e^{\prime}}\in\bigcup_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}W_{i}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\Sigma}
of the

\mathsf{Forwarding}
signature (
Fig.

7
) where
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e^{\prime}}
is
populated with at least one rep event from its event structure
(
Fig.

6
), if
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e\vartriangleleft e^{\prime}}
holds (where
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}

follows the definition from
Fig.

7
) then there exists

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e_{r}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e^{\prime}_{r}}
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e_{r}\vartriangleleft e^{\prime}_{r}}
holds, with

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e_{r}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e^{\prime}_{r}}
belonging to
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e^{\prime}}

respectively.

Proof.

We can view
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e\vartriangleleft e^{\prime}}
as a chain of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft_{1}}
relations
connecting
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e^{\prime}}
; that is,

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e\vartriangleleft_{1}\cdots\vartriangleleft_{1}e^{\prime}}
, where by definition

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft_{1}}=({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\sqsubset}\cup{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\prec})=({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\sqsubset}\cup{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wr}}$}}}$}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}})
. It is easy to see that each abs
event in this chain is populated with a rep event. Indeed, if an abs
event’s relation
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft_{1}}
to its successor is realized by

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\sqsubset}
, then the event is terminated and all of its rep events
are executed (and by our axioms, each abs event must have at least
one rep event). Alternatively, if the abs event’s relation

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft_{1}}
to its successor is realized by
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\prec}
, then the
event must be populated as per the definitions of reads-from and
writing visibility in
Fig.

7
, and in the
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}}

case by
Fig.

7
. This leaves out
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e^{\prime}}
, which has no
successor, but
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e^{\prime}}
is populated by assumption.

Now the proof is by induction on the length of the above chain. The
base case is when
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e\vartriangleleft_{1}e^{\prime}}
.
If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e\sqsubset e^{\prime}}
, the proof is by
Eq.

RB.2
giving
us
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e_{r}\sqsubset e^{\prime}_{r}}
for arbitrary
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e_{r}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}e^{\prime}_{r}}

belonging to
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e^{\prime}}
respectively. If

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wr}}$}}}$}}}e^{\prime}}
, the proof is by the definition of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wr}}$}}}$}}}}
in
Fig.

7
, giving us

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
with
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}}
. For the last case,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}e^{\prime}}
, we
have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e^{\prime}}
with either

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}
or

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma^{\prime}}}
. The former case directly exhibits rep
events that satisfy our goal. The latter does so as well, since by

Fig.

7
, it must be
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{\sigma^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
.
The inductive step is similar, with the addition that we need
transitivity of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\vartriangleleft}
and
Fig.

7
to join
events.
∎

Lemma 3.2.

Histories satisfying
\mathsf{Forwarding}
signature (
Fig.

7
)
also satisfy
\mathsf{Snapshot}
signature.

Proof sketch.

We start by determining the instantiations of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathbb{W}_{i}}
,

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\prec}
for the
\mathsf{Snapshot}
signature.
Since the effect of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
is observable only after the
execution of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
, we let

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}\in{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathbb{W}_{i}}
iff
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
is defined. For
the other notions, we first define a helper visibility over scans

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{sc}}$}}}$}}}}
, which we use to define
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\prec}
. The highlighted
relations
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{rf}$}}$}}}$}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{wr}$}}$}}}$}}}}
originate from the

\mathsf{Forwarding}
signature.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\quad\triangleq}\hphantom{{}\implies{}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{rf}$}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{sc}}$}}}$}}}s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\quad\triangleq}\hphantom{{}\implies{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}\sqsubset{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s^{\prime}}^{\bullet}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\prec}

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\quad\triangleq}\hphantom{{}\implies{}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{wr}$}}$}}}$}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{sc}}$}}}$}}}}

Eq.

V.1
holds since if
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e\prec^{+}e^{\prime}}
and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e^{\prime}\sqsubseteq e}
, then we can construct a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\vartriangleleft}
-cycle of rep
events with
Lemma

3.1
, contradicting irreflexivity of

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\vartriangleleft}
(
Lemma

2.1
).
Fig.

5
holds directly
by
Fig.

7
.
Fig.

5
holds because by the
definition of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{rf}$}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
in
\mathsf{Forwarding}
signature,
it is either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}
for which

Fig.

3
ensures uniqueness, or

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
where
Fig.

7
ensures
uniqueness.
Fig.

5
of total ordering on effectful
writes follows because in
\mathsf{Forwarding}
signature,

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{wr}$}}$}}}$}}}w^{\prime}_{i}}
is defined as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
, and the rep writes are totally
ordered by
Fig.

3
.
Fig.

5
that all
terminated writes are effectful holds because for every terminated
write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
we must have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
defined.

We next prove
Fig.

5
. We sketch the proof by
assuming there is a write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}}
such that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}\vartriangleleft s}
where
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
, and then
deriving a contradiction. Unfolding the definition of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}

in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
, we get
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{rf}$}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
where

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}={\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}
. Also, from
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\vartriangleleft s}
, using

Fig.

7
, it turns out that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\vartriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
, and moreover, the latter splits into
two possible cases:
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\sqsubset\trianglelefteqslant\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}
. The full proof of how the
cases follow from the premise and that they exhaust the
possibilities is given in Appendix

. Note that the proposition

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\sqsubset\trianglelefteqslant\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
is the key part of

Figs.

and
7
, while

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}
is the key part of

Fig.

7
; in fact, this exhaustion of cases of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\vartriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
is what let us rediscover and
mathematically formulate the forwarding principles.
The proof now proceeds by analyzing
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{rf}$}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
.
From the definition of reads-from visibility in
Fig.

7
,
we also get two cases:

({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}\land{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}})
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
, for a total of
four cases when combined with the above. In each case, contradiction
follows easily, relying in different cases on a different subset of

Figs.

7
,
7
,
7
and
3
.

Finally, we prove
Fig.

5
;
that is,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i},w_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s^{\prime}}

with
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j}\vartriangleleft w_{j}}
lead to
contradiction. As before, we first transform the assumptions

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i},w_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s^{\prime}}
into

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i},w_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma^{\prime}}}
, where

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}={\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s^{\prime}}^{\bullet}}
.
Next, by property
Fig.

7
, the virtual scans are
totally ordered by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sqsubset}
, thus either
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma\sqsubset\sigma^{\prime}}

or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma^{\prime}\sqsubset\sigma}
(the case when
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}={\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma^{\prime}}

contradicts reads-from uniqueness
Fig.

5
). In the first
case (the second is symmetric), we have

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j}\vartriangleleft w_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma\sqsubset\sigma^{\prime}}}
, and thus also

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j}\vartriangleleft w_{j}\vartriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma^{\prime}}}
. In other words, we have a
scan
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma^{\prime}}
observing a write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j}}
with another
intervening write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}}
showing up in between. But this
contradicts the argument we carried out for

Fig.

5
above.
∎

naturally follows.

Lemma

: If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\sqsubset\trianglelefteqslant\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}\vartriangleleft{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
.

We focus here on proving
Fig.

7
which involves showing if

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\sqsubset\trianglelefteqslant\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}

then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}
.
By Lemma
C.2
, we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{off}}\not\vartriangleleft{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}\vartriangleleft{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}}
was
executed. Consider if we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{on}}\vartriangleleft{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{x}}
, then
by Lemma
C.1
we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{on}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{x}}

which in turn implies that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}}
must have executed,
however that contradicts
Fig.

3
by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}}
occurring in between

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
, thus we must have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{on}}\not\vartriangleleft{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{x}}
. By
Eq.

RB.1
over

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\sqsubset{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{x}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{on}}\sqsubset{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}
, since

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{on}}\not\vartriangleleft{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{x}}
contradicts

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{on}}\sqsubset{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{x}}
, we must have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\sqsubset{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}
, which is our goal.
∎

4.
Multi-Writer Algorithms

To allow for correct non-blocking algorithms with multiple concurrent
writers, as well as for multiple concurrent scanners in the case of

Algorithm

3
, Jayanti uses
\mathbf{LL}
(load-link),
\mathbf{SC}

(store-conditional) and
\mathbf{VL}
(validate) operations

(
Jensen
et al. 1987
)
in addition to simple memory mutation and
dereference. We thus need to extend the register signature to account
for the new operations. Additionally,
Algorithms

2
and
3
, both
being multi-writer algorithms share more structure than what we
capture in the
\mathsf{Forwarding}
signature of
Fig.

7
. We thus
introduce a new
\mathsf{MWForwarding}
signature to capture the commonality of
the multi-writer algorithms. We then show that histories satisfying

\mathsf{MWForwarding}
also satisfy
\mathsf{Forwarding}
and that
Algorithms

2
and
3

satisfy
\mathsf{MWForwarding}
.

4.1.
LL/SC Registers

For a memory cell
\mathtt{X}
,
\mathbf{LL}(\mathtt{X})
, or load-link, reads from,
and returns the value of
\mathtt{X}
. It also records the time of the
read, for use in future
\mathbf{SC}
and
\mathbf{VL}
operations of the same
thread.

\mathbf{SC}(\mathtt{X},v)
, or store-conditional, writes
v
into
\mathtt{X}
and
returns
\mathtt{true}
if no other write into
\mathtt{X}
occurred since the most
recent
\mathbf{LL}(\mathtt{X})
from the same thread. Otherwise,
\mathbf{SC}

returns
\mathtt{false}
, keeping
\mathtt{X}
unchanged. Finally,
\mathbf{VL}(\mathtt{X})
,
or validate, returns truth values identically to
\mathbf{SC}
, but does
not mutate
\mathtt{X}
.

signature

\mathsf{LLReg}

\triangleq

\mathbf{LL}
/
\mathbf{SC}
Register

Let

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}W},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}R},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{LL}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{SC}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{VL}}

Set of all
writes, reads,
LL
,
SC
, and
VL
operations

successful
SC
and

operations

Figure 8
.
Signature for atomic
register with
\mathbf{LL}
/
\mathbf{SC}
/
\mathbf{VL}
operations.

To capture the properties of memory with the
\mathbf{LL}
,
\mathbf{SC}
and

\mathbf{VL}
operations, we introduce a new signature in

Fig.

8
, which we call
\mathbf{LL}
/
\mathbf{SC}
registers, or

\mathsf{LLReg}
for short. It extends
\mathsf{AReg}
from
Fig.

3
.
We will treat each execution of
\mathbf{LL}
,
\mathbf{SC}
and
\mathbf{VL}
as a
read-like event, i.e., they will observe some write by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}
. Additionally, we denote the
LL
visibility

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{ll}}$}}}$}}}}
to link
\mathbf{LL}
events with their corresponding

\mathbf{SC}
/
\mathbf{VL}
events. To capture the concept of
\mathbf{SC}
/
\mathbf{VL}

operations being successful, we introduce the set of successful events

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathcal{S}}
. If an
\mathbf{SC}
event is in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathcal{S}}
, it was
successful and must have written to memory, therefore we treat it as a
write-like event. These sets are formally defined in the top part of

Fig.

8
.

Further, referring to
Fig.

8
, the
Properties (
8
) to (
8
) are
almost identical to (
3
) to (
3
) from

\mathsf{AReg}
from
Fig.

3
. The main difference is that we
substitute quantification over writes and reads with quantification
over write-like events (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}W_{c}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}W}\cup({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{SC}}\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathcal{S}})
) and read-like events (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}R_{c}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}R}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{LL}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{SC}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{VL}}
), respectively. We also split

Fig.

3
into three: (
8
) says that each
terminated read-like event has some observed write-like event;
(
8
) says that the output of reads and
\mathbf{LL}
is the
same as the input of the observed write-like event, and
(
8
) says that the output of
\mathbf{SC}
and
\mathbf{VL}
depends
on its success.
Additionally,
Fig.

8
states that each
\mathbf{SC}
and

\mathbf{VL}
event has a terminated
\mathbf{LL}
event it is related to in

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{ll}}$}}}$}}}}
.
Fig.

8
says that each

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{ll}}$}}}$}}}}
pair has the same abs parent, capturing that the two
events of the pair were executed in the same thread (as per our
assumption in Section
2.2.1
that each event is
single-threaded), and that no other
\mathbf{LL}
event occurs in between
the pair.
Finally,

Fig.

8
captures that successful events observe the
same write-like event as their
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{ll}}$}}}$}}}}
-related
\mathbf{LL}
event,
meaning no other modifications occurred between the
\mathbf{LL}
event and
the successful event.

Figure 9
.
Illustration of key properties of
\mathbf{LL}
/
\mathbf{SC}
.

There are several important properties of
\mathbf{LL}/\mathbf{SC}/\mathbf{VL}
that
the algorithms relies upon for synchronization. We codify them in the
following three lemmas and illustrate in
Fig.

9
. For
each of the lemmas, the events
l
,
c
,
w
, and variants, operate
over the same memory cell
\mathtt{X}:\mathsf{LLReg}
.

Lemma 4.1.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{ll}}$}}}$}}}c}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{ll}}$}}}$}}}c^{\prime}}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{T}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{SC}}\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathcal{S}})
. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c\vartriangleleft c^{\prime}}
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c\vartriangleleft l^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

By
Fig.

8
, we know that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l^{\prime}}

are terminated, and by
Fig.

8
, we have that each of

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l^{\prime}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c^{\prime}}
observes some
write-like event. By
Fig.

8
with

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathcal{S}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c}

observe the same write-like event, and dually for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l^{\prime}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c^{\prime}}
, i.e., there exists
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w^{\prime}}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}l}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}c}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}l^{\prime}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}c^{\prime}}
. By
Fig.

3
, we either have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w^{\prime}\vartriangleleft c}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c\trianglelefteqslant w^{\prime}}
. In the former case, we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w^{\prime}\vartriangleleft c\vartriangleleft c^{\prime}}
, which contradicts
Fig.

3

by there being a
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c}
in between
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}c^{\prime}}
. Thus, we
must have the latter case, giving us
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c\trianglelefteqslant w^{\prime}}
, letting us
construct
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c\trianglelefteqslant w^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}l^{\prime}}
which implies our goal

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c\vartriangleleft l^{\prime}}
.
∎

Lemma 4.2.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{ll}}$}}}$}}}c}
with either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c}\in\mathcal{T}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{SC}})
or

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c}\in\mathcal{T}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{VL}}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathcal{S}})
. Then there
exists some
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}W_{c}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\not\vartriangleleft l}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\trianglelefteqslant c}
.

Proof.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c}\in\mathcal{T}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{SC}}\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathcal{S}})
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c}

must have successfully written; thus
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w}
trivially
satisfies
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\trianglelefteqslant c}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\not\vartriangleleft l}
. The latter
holds, because otherwise we get
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c\vartriangleleft l}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l\trianglelefteqslant c}
and thus
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c\vartriangleleft c}
which contradicts
irreflexivity of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\vartriangleleft}
. Next, consider

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c}\notin{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathcal{S}}
, where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c}\in\mathcal{T}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{SC}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{VL}})
.

By
Fig.

8
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l}
is terminated, and by

Fig.

8
, for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c}
there exist
write-like events
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w^{\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}W_{c}}
observed by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c}
, respectively. By
Fig.

8
,
these write-like events must be distinct, because otherwise

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c}
would have been successful. In other words,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}l}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}c}
where

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w^{\prime}}
. Taking
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w^{\prime}}
to be the required write
of the lemma, we trivially have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w^{\prime}\trianglelefteqslant c}
. To show that
also
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w^{\prime}\not\vartriangleleft l}
, we assume
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w^{\prime}\vartriangleleft l}
and derive a
contradiction. By
Fig.

3
, we either have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w^{\prime}\vartriangleleft w}
. In the first case, we
contradict
Fig.

8
for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}l}
by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}\vartriangleleft l}
. In the second case, we contradict

Fig.

8
for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}c}
by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w^{\prime}\vartriangleleft w\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}l\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{ll}}$}}}$}}}c}
.
∎

Lemma 4.3.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{ll}}$}}}$}}}c\sqsubset l^{\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{ll}}$}}}$}}}c^{\prime}}
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{T}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{SC}})
. If within the time frame of the events
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l^{\prime}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c^{\prime}}
there are no mutations to
register
\mathtt{X}
except by
\mathbf{SC}
, then there exist some

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l^{\prime\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c^{\prime\prime}}
(over
\mathtt{X}
) with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l^{\prime\prime}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{ll}}$}}}$}}}c^{\prime\prime}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l^{\prime\prime}\not\vartriangleleft l}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c^{\prime\prime}\trianglelefteqslant c^{\prime}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c^{\prime\prime}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{SC}}\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathcal{S}}
.

Proof.

From the assumption, by
Lemma

4.2
, there exists
some
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c_{0}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c^{\prime}_{0}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c_{0}\not\vartriangleleft l}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c_{0}\trianglelefteqslant c}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c^{\prime}_{0}\not\vartriangleleft l^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c^{\prime}_{0}\trianglelefteqslant c^{\prime}}
. Since there are no other writes, we can only have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c_{0}},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c^{\prime}_{0}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{SC}}\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathcal{S}}
, by

Fig.

8
there has to be some
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l^{\prime}_{0}}
such that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l^{\prime}_{0}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{ll}}$}}}$}}}c^{\prime}_{0}}
. Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l^{\prime}_{0}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c^{\prime}_{0}}
be the
existentials, we trivially have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c^{\prime}_{0}\trianglelefteqslant c^{\prime}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c^{\prime}_{0}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{SC}}\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathcal{S}}
. For
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l^{\prime}_{0}\not\vartriangleleft l}
, assume we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}l^{\prime}_{0}\vartriangleleft l}
, we derive a
contradiction. We must have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c_{0}\vartriangleleft c^{\prime}_{0}}
, since we otherwise
we contradict
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c^{\prime}_{0}\not\vartriangleleft l^{\prime}}
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c^{\prime}_{0}\vartriangleleft c_{0}\trianglelefteqslant c\sqsubset l^{\prime}}
, meaning we also must have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c_{0}\vartriangleleft l^{\prime}_{0}}
for

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c^{\prime}_{0}}
to be successful, however this contradicts
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c_{0}\not\vartriangleleft l}
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}c_{0}\vartriangleleft l^{\prime}_{0}\vartriangleleft l}
.
∎

Lemma

4.1
says that if we have two successful

\mathbf{LL}
/
\mathbf{SC}
pairs, then the intervals of the two pairs do not
overlap. To see the intuition behind the other two lemmas, consider
the special case when the order
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\vartriangleleft}
is total (e.g., it is a
linearization order), so that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\not\vartriangleleft}
is equivalent to

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\trianglerighteqslant}
.
In that case,
Lemma

4.2
says that an

\mathbf{LL}
/
\mathbf{SC}
interval, or an
\mathbf{LL}
/
\mathbf{VL}
interval with a
failing
\mathbf{VL}
, must contain some succesful write-like event.
Similarly,
Lemma

4.3
says there exists a successful

\mathbf{LL}/\mathbf{SC}
interval contained within the interval defined by two
sequential
\mathbf{LL}/\mathbf{SC}
intervals, even if the latter two intervals
themselves correspond to failing
\mathbf{SC}
’s.
The last property will be used by multi-writer algorithms as follows.
Referring to
Algorithm

2
, a write procedure will try to execute
forwarding by invoking two sequential intervals of
\mathbf{LL}/\mathbf{SC}

pairs into the cell
\mathtt{B}[i]
. Even if both
\mathbf{SC}
’s fail,

Lemma

4.3
guarantees that another concurrent write will
have invoked a successful
\mathbf{LL}/\mathbf{SC}
interval, thus forwarding
the same value on behalf of the original write.
