Figure 11
.
Signatures for multi-writer
snapshot data-structure with
\mathbf{LL}
/
\mathbf{SC}
forwarding
(
Algorithms

2
and
3
).

4.2.1.
Description of Algorithm
2

We first discuss
Algorithm

2
, which will motivate the definition
of
\mathsf{MWForwarding}
signature.
The common aspect with
Algorithm

1
is that the writer communicates
its value to the data structure by writing into
\mathtt{A}[i]
. Whereas

Algorithm

1
may try to communicate the same value
again
by
forwarding to
\mathtt{B}[i]
, in
Algorithm

2
, the forwarding procedure
reads whichever value is currently present in
\mathtt{A}[i]
and attempts
to forward it to
\mathtt{B}[i]
by means of
\mathbf{LL}
and
\mathbf{SC}
. Because
multiple writes may be racing on
\mathtt{A}[i]
, the forwarding procedure
may read and forward a different value from
\mathtt{A}[i]
than the one
the writer initially wrote. The forwarding procedure may even fail to
forward anything. Nevertheless,
Lemma

4.3
provides a
guarantee that there will exist
some
successful forwarding
among the concurrent processes, forwarding a value that is current
(i.e., written by another overlapping write). This is the key property
facilitating linearizability.

Other than that, the
scan
procedure is mostly the same in

Algorithm

2
compared to
Algorithm

1
; the main difference is
that the order between
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{on}}}
and each
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}
is
swapped, which is done in preparation for
Algorithm

3
.
In
Algorithm

1
this swap would result in a bug, causing
interference between forwarding and initialization of
\mathtt{B}
; the
writes may fail to forward their value because the forwarding flag is
turned on too late, or scans may erase forwarded values by the
initializations. But this is safe to do in
Algorithm

2
because the
use of
\mathbf{LL}
,
\mathbf{SC}
and
\mathbf{VL}
over
\mathtt{X}
and
\mathtt{B}

ensures that no forwarding (intended for a scan
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s^{\prime}}
prior to

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
) can succeed in-between
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{on}}}
of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
. This is substantiated by the following
lemma.

Lemma 4.4.

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s^{\prime}}
be a scan prior to
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
, i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s^{\prime}\sqsubset s}
. If
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{x}}}
succeeds observing the same

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s^{\prime}}_{\mathit{on}}}
that the
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{x}}
preceding

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{x}}}
observed, i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s^{\prime}}_{\mathit{on}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{x}}}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s^{\prime}}_{\mathit{on}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{x}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}\vartriangleleft{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}
must fail to write.

Proof.

We assume that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}
is successful, and derive
contradiction. By
Fig.

8
, there exists some write-like
event
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}}
, and by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}
being successful,
Fig.

8
implies
that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}
observes the same write-like event as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}}
, i.e.
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}
. By

Fig.

8
, we either have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}\vartriangleleft w}
or

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\trianglelefteqslant{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}
, in the latter case we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\vartriangleleft{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}\vartriangleleft{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}
, contradicting

Fig.

8
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}
being in between

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}w\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}
, thus we can only have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}\trianglelefteqslant w}
. Since
Algorithm

2
is single-scanner,
it follows that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
only starts after
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s^{\prime}}
has finished,
thus we can derive the following:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s^{\prime}}_{\mathit{on}}\sqsubset{s^{\prime}}_{\mathit{off}}\sqsubset{s}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}\trianglelefteqslant w\vartriangleleft{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}\vartriangleleft{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{x}}}

which implies that we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s^{\prime}}_{\mathit{off}}}
in between

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s^{\prime}}_{\mathit{on}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{x}}}
, contradicting
Fig.

8
.
∎

4.2.2.
The
\mathsf{MWForwarding}
Signature

The rep-event signatures for multi-writer algorithms in

Fig.

10
correspond closely to the rep events of

Algorithm

2
.

Fig.

10
extends
Fig.

6
, adding the

\mathbf{LL}/\mathbf{SC}
register
\mathtt{X}
and extending the event signatures.

\textsc{write}_{i}
+ extends
\textsc{write}_{i}
by events
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{x}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\mathit{f_{1}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\mathit{f_{2}}}
from
Algorithm

2
, along
with an event signature
\textsc{forward}_{i}
corresponding to the
procedure
forward
from
Algorithm

2
.

vscan
+ extends
vscan
by updating
\mathtt{B}
to be an array
of
\mathbf{LL}/\mathbf{SC}
registers, and adding four additional rep events:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{on}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{off}}}
corresponding to

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{on}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{off}}}
from
Algorithm

2
, and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{\underline{on}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{\underline{off}}}}
which are providing
support for multi-scanner
Algorithm

3
.
As we shall see in Section
4.3
, in
Algorithm

3
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{on}}}
might not return before
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}
, and
similarly for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{off}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
. Thus, we
need events that observe
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{on}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{off}}}

returning before
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}

respectively. For
Algorithm

2
, we simply take

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{\underline{on}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{on}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{\underline{off}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{off}}}
.

Signature
\mathsf{MWForwarding}
in
Fig.

11
captures the common
structure of
Algorithms

2
and
3
. Some aspects are preserved from

Algorithm

1
; for instance

Figs.

7
,
7
,
7
,
7
and
7

are the same as in
\mathsf{Forwarding}
signature of
Fig.

7
.
One difference is that we now have sets of forwarding events

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}F_{i}}
that are instances of the forwarding procedure. The new
signature introduces operations over such forwardings:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}-}]_{w}}
maps a forwarding to the write that invoked it,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}[{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}-}]_{\sigma}}
maps a forwarding to the virtual scan it
is forwarding to. Forwarding visibility
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}}
is also updated:
it now says that a forwarding reads a value of a write from

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
and relays this value by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}
.

Among the new properties, (
11
) encodes that only

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}
performs forwarding writes into

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}.\mathtt{B}[i]
.
Fig.

11
ensures that nothing
wrote to
\mathtt{X}
other than
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{off}}}
directly after

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{on}}}
.

Figs.

11
,
11
and
11
corresponds
to the rep event order of scans, writes and forwarding respectively.
Lastly,
Fig.

11
ensures that every forwarding has a
virtual scan to forward to, while
Fig.

11
states that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}
can only be performed if
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{x}}}
observed

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{on}}}
.

Lemma 4.5.

Histories satisfying
\mathsf{MWForwarding}
signature also satisfy
\mathsf{Forwarding}

signature.

Proof sketch.

We instantiate
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\Sigma}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}[-]^{\bullet}}
of
\mathsf{Forwarding}
to be the
same as the corresponding instantiations of
\mathsf{MWForwarding}
.

Figs.

7
,
7
,
7
,
7
and
7

are shared between the signatures, therefore they trivially hold.

We next focus on proving
Fig.

7
,
leaving the remaining properties for Appendix
D
.
Proving
Fig.

7
requires showing that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\sqsubset\trianglelefteqslant\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}

and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}}
derive a contradiction. To show this, we
need a helper lemma, proved in Appendix
D
(Lemma
D.2
):
If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\sqsubset\trianglelefteqslant\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
then surely
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
will
be able to forward to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}
in time, i.e., we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{off}}\not\vartriangleleft{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{x}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{off}}\not\vartriangleleft{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{x}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}\vartriangleleft{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
for every
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}f}
executed by

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
.
From
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
, by the definition of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{fwd}}$}}}$}}}}
, we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{a}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}f}
. By

Lemma

3.1
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}}
implies

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
.
We can also infer (full proof in
Appendix
D
) that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{on}}\vartriangleleft{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{x}}
.

By Lemma
D.2
, we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{off}}\not\vartriangleleft{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{x}}
, thus by

Fig.

11
we derive

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{on}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{x}}
, implying
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\mathit{f_{1}}}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\mathit{f_{2}}}
will be executed, let

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}f^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\mathit{f_{1}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}f^{\prime\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\mathit{f_{2}}}
.
By Lemma
D.2
, we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{off}}\not\vartriangleleft{f^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{x}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{off}}\not\vartriangleleft{f^{\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{x}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f^{\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}\vartriangleleft{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
, similarly to above with

Fig.

11
we derive

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{on}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{f^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{x}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{on}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{f^{\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{x}}}
, meaning
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f^{\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}
will be executed, meaning we have two
consecutive
\mathbf{LL}
/
\mathbf{SC}
pairs of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}f^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}f^{\prime\prime}}
.
Since we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{on}}\vartriangleleft{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{x}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f^{\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}\vartriangleleft{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
, it is not possible for any

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{r}_{i}}}
to occur in the intervals of the

\mathbf{LL}
/
\mathbf{SC}
pairs, therefore we can apply

Lemma

4.3
, thus there exists some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}f^{\prime\prime\prime}}
such
that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f^{\prime\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}\not\vartriangleleft{f^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f^{\prime\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}\trianglelefteqslant{f^{\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}
.

By
Fig.

8
, we either have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}\vartriangleleft{f^{\prime\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}
or

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f^{\prime\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}\trianglelefteqslant{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}
. In the first case, we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}\vartriangleleft{f^{\prime\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}\trianglelefteqslant{f^{\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}\vartriangleleft{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}

which contradicts
Fig.

8
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f^{\prime\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}

occurring in between
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
. In the
second case, if we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f^{\prime\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}\vartriangleleft{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}
then by

Lemma

4.1
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f^{\prime\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}

being successful, we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f^{\prime\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}\vartriangleleft{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}}
, and
this also follows in the case of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f^{\prime\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}={f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}
.
By
Eq.

RB.1
over
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\sqsubset{f^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f^{\prime\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}\sqsubset{f^{\prime\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}
we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\sqsubset{f^{\prime\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}}
since we cannot have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f^{\prime\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}\sqsubset{f^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}}
by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{f^{\prime\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}\not\vartriangleleft{f^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}}
. Thus, we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\sqsubset{f^{\prime\prime\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{b}}\vartriangleleft{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{b}\sqsubset{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{a}}}

which contradicts
Fig.

3
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}

occurring in between
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{f}\hskip-1.0pt\mathit{\underline{a}}}
.
∎

Lemma 4.6.

Every execution of
Algorithm

2
satisfies the
\mathsf{MWForwarding}
signature
(
Fig.

11
).

Proof.

Since this algorithm is single scanner, we can simply define the set
of virtual scans to be the same as the set of abs scans and map each
abs scan to itself, i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\Sigma}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}S}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
. Each rep event directly corresponds to
their equivalent variant in
Algorithm

2
, except for

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{\underline{on}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{\underline{off}}}}
, which are set as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{\underline{on}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{on}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{on}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{\underline{off}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}_{\mathit{off}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{off}}}
.

Fig.

7
holds by each event being a
subevent of itself (
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e\subseteq e}
) and
Fig.

7

holds since the algorithm is single-scanner.
Properties (
7
) to (
11
) and
(
11
) to (
11
) holds directly
by the structure of the algorithm and
Fig.

11
can be
proven by Lemma
C.1
, the same lemma that established this
property for
Algorithm

1
, since the relative structure of

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{on}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s}_{\mathit{off}}}
is the same.
∎

4.3.

Algorithm 3:
Jayanti’s multi-writer, multi-scanner snapshot algorithm.

Algorithm

3
is the final of the Jayanti’s snapshot algorithms,
allowing unconstrained write and scan operations. It differs from

Algorithm

2
in the implementation of
scan
, while

write
is mostly unchanged.
While
Algorithm

3
allows for multiple concurrently running
physical scanners, under the hood, at most one
virtual
scanner
can be logically viewed as running at any point in time.
The physical scans maintain their own local snapshot and forwarding
arrays, but the shared resource
\mathtt{X}
, via the fields
\mathtt{X}.\mathsf{p}_{\mathtt{A}}

and
\mathtt{X}.\mathsf{p}_{\mathtt{B}}
, keeps track which local snapshot array and local
forwarding array, respectively, should be considered as the current
data of the virtual scan. The physical scanners race to modify these
fields in order to promote their own local arrays as the arrays of the
virtual scan. However,
\mathtt{X}
can be modified only by
\mathbf{SC}
; thus,
by
Lemma

4.1
, mutations to
\mathtt{X}
occur sequentially, and
by
Lemma

4.2
, at least one mutation exists. The
sequential nature of these mutations justifies viewing them as
belonging to a single, albeit virtual, scan. Once the virtual scan
terminates, a snapshot is copied into
\mathtt{SS}
(
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{v}_{\mathit{\underline{ss}}}}
), which
scanners read (
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}s_{\mathit{ss}}}
) and return. We follow Jayanti in assuming
that
\mathtt{SS}
, even though physically a compound object, can still be
considered an
\mathbf{LL}
/
\mathbf{SC}
register; this is not a loss of
generality, as multi-word
\mathbf{LL}
/
\mathbf{SC}
registers
exist
(
Jayanti and
Petrovic 2005
)
.

The
pushVS
procedure (short for “push virtual scan”)
propels the virtual scanner to its completion, producing a snapshot in

\mathtt{SS}
.
The procedure is divided into three phases, tracked by the
field
\mathtt{X}.\mathtt{phase}
, corresponding to the three phases of the
previous algorithms: the first phase sets each memory cell in
\mathtt{B}

to
\bot
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{vr}_{i}}
; the second phase reads the main memory

\mathtt{A}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{va}_{i}}
; and the third phase reads the forwarding
memory
\mathtt{B}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{vb}_{i}}
. However, unlike before when

\mathtt{B}
was a global array, here we have one
\mathtt{B}_{p}
for each scan
process
p
. A scanner that successfully writes with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{v}_{\mathit{on}}}
in
the first phase will have its
\mathtt{B}_{p}
used for forwarding in the
current virtual scan. For the second phase, the algorithm uses

\mathtt{A}_{p}
to store the results of reading
\mathtt{A}
, and similarly the
process
p
which successfully writes with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{v}_{\mathit{off}}}
will have
its
\mathtt{A}_{p}
used for the next phase. The third phase uses
\mathtt{A}_{p}

and
\mathtt{B}_{q}
from the processes stored in
\mathtt{X}
to construct the
snapshot, which is then stored in
\mathtt{SS}
with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{v}_{\mathit{\underline{ss}}}}
.

Each phase starts with an
\mathbf{LL}(\mathtt{X})
(three lines labeled

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{vx}}
) and ends with an
\mathbf{SC}(\mathtt{X})
. This ensures that if
multiple physical scanners run a phase simultaneously, only a single

\mathbf{SC}
operation succeeds writing to
\mathtt{X}
, committing the results
of the phase. Thus, considered across all physical scanners,
successful phases cannot overlap, by
Lemma

4.1
. Also, phases
must run consecutively, because a phase in some scanner can only start
if the previous phase, maybe executed by some other scanner, has been
terminated, incrementing
\mathtt{X}.\mathtt{phase}
to enable the next phase
(
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{v}_{\mathit{on}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{v}_{\mathit{off}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{v}_{\mathit{end}}}
).
Because the phases cannot overlap, must be executed in order, and
correspond to the scanner phases in the previous algorithms, a trace of
a virtual scan in
Algorithm

3
has essentially the same structure
as a trace of a physical scan in the
Algorithms

1
and
2
.

To ensure that
\mathtt{SS}
is only mutated once per virtual scan, we have
two toggle bits
\mathtt{X}.\mathtt{sync}
and
\mathtt{SS}.\mathtt{sync}
as part of

\mathtt{X}
and
\mathtt{SS}
respectively. These function such that every time

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{v}_{\mathit{end}}}
successfully writes,
\mathtt{X}.\mathtt{sync}
is negated.

\mathtt{SS}
can only be written to by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{v}_{\mathit{\underline{ss}}}}
, which can only be
executed if
\mathtt{X}.\mathtt{sync}
and
\mathtt{SS}.\mathtt{sync}
are
equal
1
1

1

Jayanti’s original presentation is dual, as the sync
bits need to be distinct for writing to
\mathtt{SS}
, but this is
equivalent.
and
\mathtt{X}
did not change during the phase. Once

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{v}_{\mathit{\underline{ss}}}}
successfully writes,
\mathtt{SS}.\mathtt{sync}
will be negated,
meaning no more writes into
\mathtt{SS}
can occur in this phase since

\mathtt{X}.\mathtt{sync}
and
\mathtt{SS}.\mathtt{sync}
are now distinct. We also know

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{v}_{\mathit{\underline{ss}}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{v}_{\mathit{\underline{ss}}}}

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{v}_{\mathit{\underline{ss}}}}
succeeded, or some other
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}\mathit{v}_{\mathit{end}}}
ended the
phase.

It is also vital for linearizability that the virtual scan is in the
time interval of the abs
scan
returning it. To ensure the
virtual scan ends before the abs scanner ends, the latest virtual scan
is finished before
pushVS
terminates. Dually, to ensure that
the virtual scan was not started before the abs scanner starts,

pushVS
is executed twice; once to finish the currently running
virtual scan, and once to generate a virtual scan that can be used by
the abs scanner. Executing
pushVS
twice is similar to the idea of
executing
forward
twice in
Algorithm

2
.

(

).

5.
Applying to Other Snapshot Algorithms

To show the generality of our approach, we next apply it to the
snapshot algorithm of
Afek et al. 1993
, presented as

Algorithm

4
. It is a single-writer, multi-scanner algorithm.
At its core,
scan
of
Algorithm

4
collects snapshots by
reading the memory array
\mathtt{A}
twice. We denote the first read of
index
i
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s_{k}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}
, and the second by

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s_{k}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
. The algorithm looks for changes in the array. If
a change is detected, the reading is restarted in the next
iteration. The index
k
on the reading events identifies the
iteration in which the event occurs. If no change to the array is
detected between the first and second read events, then what was read
is a valid snapshot, reflecting what was in the array at the moment
the last index was first read in the current iteration.
To ensure that changes to the array are properly recognized, each
memory cell holds a version number (
\mathtt{A}[i].\mathtt{ver}
) of the
latest write, which is a number that is incremented each time a new
value is written to the cell.

1:

\mathtt{A}:\mathbf{array}[n]\ \mathbf{of}\

2:

\mathchoice{\raisebox{-14.0pt}{\resizebox{444414}{2129920}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.45876pt}{$\displaystyle<$}}}}}{\raisebox{-14.0pt}{\resizebox{444414}{2129920}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.45876pt}{$\textstyle<$}}}}}{\raisebox{-14.0pt}{\resizebox{444414}{2129920}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5543pt}{$\scriptstyle<$}}}}}{\raisebox{-14.0pt}{\resizebox{444414}{2129920}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.17593pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle<$}}}}}\,\begin{aligned} \mathtt{data}&:\mathsf{val}\\
\mathtt{ver}&:\mathbb{N}\\
\mathtt{view}&:\mathbf{array}[n]\ \mathbf{of}\ \mathsf{val}\end{aligned}\,\mathchoice{\raisebox{-14.0pt}{\resizebox{444414}{2129920}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.45876pt}{$\displaystyle>$}}}}}{\raisebox{-14.0pt}{\resizebox{444414}{2129920}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.45876pt}{$\textstyle>$}}}}}{\raisebox{-14.0pt}{\resizebox{444414}{2129920}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5543pt}{$\scriptstyle>$}}}}}{\raisebox{-14.0pt}{\resizebox{444414}{2129920}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.17593pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle>$}}}}}

3:

write
(
i:\mathbb{N},v:\mathsf{val}
)
\triangleq

\triangleright

w_{i}

4:

s\vcentcolon=\textsc{scan}()

\triangleright

{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{s}}

5:

\mathtt{A}[i]\vcentcolon=\left\langle v,\mathtt{A}[i].\mathtt{ver}+1,s\right\rangle

\triangleright

{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}

1:

scan
:

\mathbf{array}[n]\ \mathbf{of}\ \mathsf{val}

\triangleq

\triangleright

s

2:

for

i\in\left\{0\dots n-1\right\}

do

3:

\mathit{moved}[i]\vcentcolon=\mathtt{false}

4:

while

\mathtt{true}

do

k
-th iteration of loop

5:

for

i\in\left\{0\dots n-1\right\}

do

6:

a[i]\vcentcolon=\mathtt{A}[i]

\triangleright

{s_{k}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}

7:

for

i\in\left\{0\dots n-1\right\}

do

8:

b[i]\vcentcolon=\mathtt{A}[i]

\triangleright

{s_{k}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}

9:

\mathit{changed}\vcentcolon=\mathtt{false}

10:

for

i\in\left\{0\dots n-1\right\}

do

11:

if

a[i].\mathtt{ver}\neq b[i].\mathtt{ver}

then

12:

if

\mathit{moved}[i]

then

13:

return

b[i].\mathtt{view}

14:

else

15:

\mathit{changed}\vcentcolon=\mathtt{true}

16:

\mathit{moved}[i]\vcentcolon=\mathtt{true}

17:

if

\neg\mathit{changed}

then

18:

return

(b[0].\mathtt{data},\dots,b[n-1].\mathtt{data})

19:

end while

Algorithm 4:
Single-writer, multi-scanner snapshot algorithm of

Afek et al. 1993
.

If
scan
detects that some index
i
has changed twice during
scanning, it can immediately terminate, returning the
view
of
the latest write into
i
(
\mathtt{A}[i].\mathtt{view}
). The view itself is
a snapshot each
write
collects by calling
scan
before
writing its value. Keeping views ensures that the snapshot methods are
wait-free.
scan
tracks how many times index
i
has changed by
updating the local variable
\mathit{moved}[i]
each time it detects a
change. Each
write
commits its value simultaneously with
incrementing the version number and updating the view.

We next use visibility relations to show that
Algorithm

4

satisfies the
\mathsf{Snapshot}
signature, and is thus linearizable by

Lemma

2.3
.

Lemma 5.1.

Every execution of
Algorithm

4
satisfies the
\mathsf{Snapshot}

signature (
Fig.

5
).

Proof sketch.

To prove that
Algorithm

4
satisfies the
\mathsf{Snapshot}

signature, we employ virtual scans as a simplification mechanism
that allows us to elide
write
views from consideration when
establishing
\mathsf{Snapshot}
.
A virtual scan will denote a scan that detected no change in the
array, i.e., it returned at line
18
. To each abs
scan, we can associate a virtual scan as follows. If the abs scan
terminated because it detected no change, than it immediately is a
virtual scan. If the abs scan terminated by returning a view from a

write
, i.e., returned at line
13
, then that
view itself is an abs scan which can, recursively, be associated
with a virtual scan. Importantly, given abs scan
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
, the
associated virtual scan
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}
satisfies
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
, from which we shall derive the
\mathsf{Snapshot}

axioms.

Formally, a virtual scan
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}
consists of rep events

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
for each
i
, where
both
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
observe the
same write (i.e., no change detected), and every

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}
occurs before any
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{j}}}
:

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma},i.

\exists{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}.\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}\land{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}

(
A.1
)

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma},i,j.

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}\sqsubset{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{j}}}

(
A.2
)

If scan
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
returned at line
18
, we simply
define the virtual scan
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}
as the set of rep events

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s_{k}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s_{k}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
, where
k
is
the last reading iteration of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
. If scan
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
returned
at line
13
, then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
returns the view of some

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
, and we define
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{s}}})^{\bullet}}
. This is a well-founded recursive
definition, because the ending time of the scan
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{s}}}
on
the right is smaller than the ending time of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
on the left,
and the ending times are bounded from below by
0
. To see that the
ending time of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{s}}}
is below that of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
, suppose
otherwise. Then it must be
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}\sqsubset{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
, because
from the code of
write
we have that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{s}}\sqsubset{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
. In particular,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s_{k}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}\sqsubset{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
for every
k
. But, because
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
is
observed by some rep read in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
, we also have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{s_{k}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
for some
k
. Thus, we have an event

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s_{k}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
that terminated before the event

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
that it observes, which
contradicts
Eq.

V.1
.

Next, we show that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
.
If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
terminated with no changes detected, this is trivial,
since
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}
consists of the selected rep events of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
. Otherwise,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{s}}})^{\bullet}}
,
for some write view
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{s}}}
. By recursion on the
definition of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}[-]^{\bullet}}
it must be

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{s}}}^{\bullet}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{s}}}
, so
it suffices to show
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{s}}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
.
This holds because, to reach the return at line
13
,
two changes of
\mathtt{A}[i]
must have occurred, thus there are two
different writes to
\mathtt{A}[i]
before
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
that where
observed by some rep event in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
, implying
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
starts
before
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
and thus also before
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{s}}}
.
Additionally, since some
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s_{k}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
must have read

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
, i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{s_{k}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
,
it must be that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{s}}}
ends before
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
, as
otherwise
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{s_{k}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}\sqsubset{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
contradicts

Eq.

V.1
.

We next proceed to establish the
\mathsf{Snapshot}
signature. First, we
instantiate
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathbb{W}_{i}}
to be the set of all writes
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}\in{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}W_{i}}
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
is defined; these are the
writes that executed their effect. Second, we instantiate the
visibility relations
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\prec}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}
as follows,
using the helper relation
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{rf}$}}$}}}$}}}}
.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{rf}$}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\quad\triangleq}\hphantom{{}\implies{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\quad\triangleq}\hphantom{{}\implies{}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{rf}$}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\prec}

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\quad\triangleq}\hphantom{{}\implies{}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}

In English, the scan
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
reads from, and also observes,

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
iff there is an appropriate rep event in the virtual
scan
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}
that reads from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
at the level
of rep events.
We now argue that the definitions of the visibility relations
satisfy the
\mathsf{Snapshot}
properties.
Eq.

V.1
holds since
if
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e\prec^{+}e^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e^{\prime}\sqsubseteq e}
then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e\prec^{+}e^{\prime}}
can only hold if
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}e^{\prime}}
, i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}}
. But then, by
Eq.

RB.2
, from

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e^{\prime}\sqsubseteq e}
we derive
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}\sqsubset{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
,
which contradicts
Eq.

V.1
for registers.
Fig.

5

holds by the structure of the algorithm.
Fig.

5
holds
since if
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
, then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
are observed by the same
read. Thus, by
Fig.

3
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}={w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
, and since each write executes
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
only
once by Property
(ES.2)
, it must be
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}=w^{\prime}_{i}}
.
Fig.

5
holds because the algorithm is
assumed to be single-writer.
Fig.

5
holds since each
terminated write must have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
defined, which is how
we define the set
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathbb{W}_{i}}
.

Next,
Fig.

5
says that given
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
,
there exists no
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}\vartriangleleft s}
.
To prove it, we assume that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}}
exists, and derive a
contradiction. By assumption, we have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{rf}$}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}

for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}={\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}
. From

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}={\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
, and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}\vartriangleleft s}
, we can derive

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}\vartriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
, and then by

Section

5
, also

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
. We elide the
proof of the last derivation; it is similar to

Lemma

3.1
and is in
Appendix
F
. By the
definition of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{rf}$}}$}}}$}}}}
and
Section

5
,

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{rf}$}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
implies

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}},{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
. But then

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
occurrs between
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
, contradicting
Fig.

3
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
.

Lastly, we prove
Fig.

5
:
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i},w_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s^{\prime}}
with
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j}\vartriangleleft w_{j}}
lead to contradiction. We unfold the assumptions

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i},w_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s^{\prime}}
into

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i},w_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{rf}$}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i},w^{\prime}_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{rf}$}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma^{\prime}}}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}={\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma^{\prime}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s^{\prime}}^{\bullet}}
. By definition of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{rf}$}}$}}}$}}}}
and
Section

5
, from
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{rf}$}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma}}
we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{j}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{j}},{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{j}}}
, and similarly for
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{rf}$}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}\sigma^{\prime}}}
. By
Section

5
we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{j}}\sqsubset{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}\sqsubset{\sigma^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{j}}}
, and
by
Eq.

RB.1
, it is either
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{j}}\sqsubset{\sigma^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{j}}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{i}}\sqsubset{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{i}}}
. In
the first case (the second is symmetric) we can construct

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{j}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{w_{j}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}_{j}}\sqsubset{\sigma^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{j}}}
, meaning that we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{j}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{w_{j}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{\sigma^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{j}}}
contradicting

Fig.

3
for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{j}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}{\sigma^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{j}}}
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{j}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
occurring between

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{j}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{\sigma^{\prime}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{b}_{j}}}
.
∎

We conclude this section by observing that the inverse of

Lemma

2.3
also holds; that is,
\mathsf{Snapshot}
signature is
actually satisfiable by every linearizable snapshot algorithm. This
follows by instantiating
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\prec}
with the linearization order,
defining
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}
to relate each read with the latest write
before that read in the linearization, and defining
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathbb{W}_{i}}

as the set of all writes in the linearization. The axioms of the
signature then simply state straightforward properties of the
linearization order and of sequential execution in that order.
That said, we have established
\mathsf{Snapshot}
signature for

Algorithm

4

without
assuming linearizability, so that we
can derive linearizability by
Lemma

2.3
.

6.
Related Work

The idea to use visibility relations for proving linearizability has
first been proposed by
Henzinger
et al. 2013
and applied to
concurrent queue algorithms. The explicit motivation of this approach
was to modularize the linearizability proofs.
The work on time-stamped stack algorithm of
Dodds
et al. 2015
builds
on this by introducing a hybrid approach, combining the visibility
method together with the linearization points method. Visibility has
also been applied on algorithms over weak memory, where it is
typically called communication order
(
Raad et al. 2019
)
. Another
related approach of visibility by
Emmi and Enea 2019
;
Krishna
et al. 2020

formulates weakened specifications for linearizability, where all
operations do not have to linearly occur one after another in a
simulation, but occur in partial order, allowing for certain
operations to miss another.

Concerning Jayanti’s algorithms specifically,
Jayanti 2005

sketched linearizability of the first algorithm by describing its
linearization points. He also argued informally, based on forwarding
principles, that the changes between the algorithms preserve
linearizability.

Delbianco et al. 2017
gave a proof formalized in Coq of the first
algorithm, by constructing a linearization order along the execution
of the algorithm. The proof is specific to the implementation, making
it is unclear how to lift it to the other two algorithms. Based on
that development,
Jacobs 2018
developed a mechanized proof,
also of the first algorithm, in VeriFast using prophecy variables.

Petrank and
Timnat 2013
and
Timnat 2015
present an algorithm
based on the forwarding idea of Jayanti, which implements a set
interface with insert, remove and contains operations, along with an
iterator which is a generalized form of scanner. The set operations
can be forwarded to the scanner, generalizing Jayanti’s forwarding
which applies only to write operations. In the future, we will
consider how to generalize our proof to apply to this algorithm as
well. This would involve axiomatizing the set data-structure, but also
what it means abstractly to be a forwarding structure, so that both
Jayanti’s snapshot and the set structure of Petrank and Timnat are
instances.

Afek et al. 1993
proved their algorithm linearizable by using the
linearization point method. While the use of the linearization point
method is fairly straightforward for this algorithm, we showed that we
can reuse our snapshot axiomatization for its proof.

7.
Conclusion and Future Work

We presented proofs of linearizability of Jayanti’s three snapshot
algorithms developed in a modular fashion, using the method of
visibility relations. More concretely, the linearizability proofs are
decomposed into proof modules, with many of the modules being shared
between the three algorithms; they are developed once and reused three
times to reduce proof complexity.

Importantly, the module interfaces are signatures consisting of
relations and axioms on them that encode the key idea of “forwarding
principles” underpinning Jayanti’s design. We thus show that a
formalism based on visibility relations is powerful enough to
mathematically capture these principles; previously, Jayanti has only
presented them informally in English.

In the future, we plan to apply the visibility methods to other
algorithms, and potentially also use the developed modules and
signatures as guides in designing new and more efficient algorithms,
much like Jayanti’s three algorithms start with the
single-writer/single-scanner variant and build to the ultimately
desired multi-writer/multi-scanner variant. We shall also study how
the visibility method applies to non-linearizable algorithms, and
how to mechanize our proof in a proof assistant.

Acknowledgements.

We thank the anonymous reviewers from the POPL’22 PC for their
feedback. This research was partially supported by the Spanish
MICINN projects BOSCO (PGC2018-102210-B-I00) and the European
Research Council project Mathador (ERC2016-COG-724464).

Appendix A
Snapshot Signature Implies Linearizability

Here we prove that every algorithm with histories satisfying the

\mathsf{Snapshot}
signature is linearizable. This proof is based on a
similar proof for queues by
Chakraborty et al. 2015
.

Following
Definition

2.2
of linearizability, we start by choosing a
visibility relation
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\prec}
that constructs the set

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E_{c}}=\{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}\mid\exists{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{T}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E}).\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e\prec^{*}e^{\prime}}\}
, and then extend it to a linearization

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<}
. Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\prec}
be the visibility relation obtained by
restricting the visibility relation postulated by
\mathsf{Snapshot}
to

\mathcal{T}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}S})\cup\bigcup_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathbb{W}_{i}}
. Events outside of
the restriction are non-terminated scans or writes that have not
executed their effect yet; they do not modify the abstract state, and
are hence not necessary for linearization.
Additionally, by
Fig.

5
we know that every terminated
write is effectful. Thus going forward, we consider
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E_{c}}=\mathcal{T}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}S})\cup\bigcup_{i}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathbb{W}_{i}}
.

To move towards the construction of a linearization, we will first
construct a partial order
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft_{w}}
over all write events that
contains all logically implied orderings. We will in steps expand this
order, until we have a total order.

Definition A.1.

Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wrDiff}}$}}}$}}}w^{\prime}_{j}}
be defined if
i\neq j
and there exists
a write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}}
and a scan
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
such that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i},w_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{j}}
. Let

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft_{w}}\triangleq({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}\ \cup\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wrDiff}}$}}}$}}}})^{+}
.

Lemma A.2.

The relation
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft_{w}}
is a strict partial order.

Proof.

For a relation to be a strict partial order, it needs to be
transitive and irreflexive. The relation
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft_{w}}
is transitive
by definition since it is a transitive closure. We prove
irreflexivity by proving acyclicity of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft_{w1}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}\ \cup\ {\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wrDiff}}$}}}$}}}}
, i.e., we prove there
exists no cycle of the following form:

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}\vartriangleleft_{w1}w_{2}\vartriangleleft_{w1}\cdots\vartriangleleft_{w1}w_{n}\vartriangleleft_{w1}w_{1}}

We show this by showing that there exists no cycle of size
n=1

and that we can shrink any cycle of size
n\geq 2
, meaning that we
can recursively shrink a cycle until it is of size
n=1
. For a
cycle with
n=1
, we have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}\vartriangleleft_{w1}w_{1}}
, which can only be
the case
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}\vartriangleleft w_{1}}
, since two writes joined by

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wrDiff}}$}}}$}}}}
must come from distinct memory cells. However, we
cannot have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}\vartriangleleft w_{1}}
either, since it contradicts

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}
being irreflexive (
Lemma

2.1
).
We show that we can shrink any cycle of
n\geq 2
by doing a case
analysis on the first two instances of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft_{w1}}
in the cycle:

(1)

We have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}\vartriangleleft w_{2}\vartriangleleft w_{3}\vartriangleleft_{w1}^{*}w_{1}}
. By
transitivity of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}
we can shrink the cycle to the smaller
cycle
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}\vartriangleleft w_{3}\vartriangleleft_{w1}^{*}w_{1}}
.

(2)

We have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wrDiff}}$}}}$}}}w_{2}\vartriangleleft w_{3}\vartriangleleft_{w1}^{*}w_{1}}
, i.e.,

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{2}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j}}

with
i\neq j
and there exists
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}}
such that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i},w_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{j}}
and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{2}\vartriangleleft w_{3}\vartriangleleft_{w1}^{*}w_{1}}
. Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{3}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime\prime}_{k}}

where
k
can either be equal to
i
or
j
or some other distinct
index. Since each scan is terminated and thus observes the entire
memory by
Fig.

5
, there must exist some
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{k}}
such
that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{k}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
. By comparing
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{k}}
and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime\prime}_{k}}
with
Fig.

5
, we have either

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime\prime}_{k}\trianglelefteqslant w_{k}}
or
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{k}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime\prime}_{k}}
. If we have

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime\prime}_{k}\trianglelefteqslant w_{k}}
then we also have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j}\vartriangleleft s}
by

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime\prime}_{k}\trianglelefteqslant w_{k}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
, which implies that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j}}
happens between
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
,
contradicting
Fig.

5
. Thus we must have

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{k}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime\prime}_{k}}
, which lets us construct

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wrDiff}}$}}}$}}}w_{3}\vartriangleleft_{w1}w_{1}}
, giving us a shorter cycle.

(3)

We have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wrDiff}}$}}}$}}}w_{2}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wrDiff}}$}}}$}}}w_{3}\vartriangleleft_{w1}^{*}w_{1}}
, i.e.,

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{2}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j}}
and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{3}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime\prime}_{k}}
with
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i},w_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j},w^{\prime}_{k}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s^{\prime}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{j}}
and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{k}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime\prime}_{k}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{3}\vartriangleleft_{w1}^{*}w_{1}}
where

i\neq j
and
j\neq k
. Similar to the previous case, by

Fig.

5
, there must exist a
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{k}}
such that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{k}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
. By
Fig.

5
with

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j},w_{k}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j},w^{\prime}_{k}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s^{\prime}}
and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{j}}
, it must follow that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{k}\not\vartriangleleft w_{k}}
, which by
Fig.

5
implies

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{k}\trianglelefteqslant w^{\prime}_{k}}
, giving us
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{k}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime\prime}_{k}}
by

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{k}\trianglelefteqslant w^{\prime}_{k}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime\prime}_{k}}
. This lets us construct

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wrDiff}}$}}}$}}}w_{3}\vartriangleleft_{w1}^{*}w_{1}}
giving us a shorter cycle.

(4)

We have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{1}\vartriangleleft w_{2}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wrDiff}}$}}}$}}}w_{3}\vartriangleleft_{w1}^{*}w_{1}}
. For this
case we simply
rotate
the cycle to move the first instance
to the end of the cycle, i.e.,

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{2}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wrDiff}}$}}}$}}}w_{3}\vartriangleleft_{w1}^{*}w_{1}\vartriangleleft w_{2}}
, and case match on the
next two
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft_{w1}}
instances to create a scenario where we
either use case (2) or case (3). ∎

By Zorn’s Lemma, we can arbitrarily choose a total order
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}

extending
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft_{w}}
, for which we define the concept of maximal
candidate, which we use to construct the complete total order.

Definition A.3.

An event
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}
is called a maximal candidate of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}

when
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}
is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}
-maximal (i.e., there exists no greater
event in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}
, which may not necessarily be unique) and one
of the following holds:

(1)

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
is a write with no scans observing it
and it is the greatest write in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
,

(2)

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
is a scan with for all
i
, there exists

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
is the
greatest event of order
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathbb{W}_{i}}
.

Lemma A.4.

For any non-empty history satisfying the
\mathsf{Snapshot}
signature,
there exists a maximal candidate of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
.

Proof.

Since
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
is a total order over a finite set, there exists
a unique greatest write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
, consider the following cases:

(1)

If nothing reads
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
is

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}
-maximal, then
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
is trivially a maximal
candidate.

(2)

If nothing reads
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
is not

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}
-maximal, then there exists an event
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}
greater
than
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
, such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\vartriangleleft e}
. The event cannot be
a write event
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j}}
, since that would imply

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}<_{w}w^{\prime}_{j}}
, contradicting
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
being

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
-maximal. Thus it must be a scan event

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
, by
Fig.

5
there exists
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}}

such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
with
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}\neq{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}}
,
since nothing reads
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
. Since we have

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\vartriangleleft s}
, it must be the case
that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
does not happen in-between
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}}
and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
as per
Fig.

5
, i.e., we have

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\not\vartriangleleft w_{i}}
, thus by
Fig.

5
we have

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}}
. With
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
being

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
-maximal, we also have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}<_{w}w_{i}}
, however
this contradicts
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}}
.

(3)

If there exists
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
with

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
being
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}
-maximal, then we show that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}

is a maximal candidate For
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
to be a maximal candidate, we
need for all
j
, there exists
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
such that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}}
is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
-maximal in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathbb{W}_{j}}
. By

Fig.

5
, we know that there exists some
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}}
such
that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
. Suppose for each
j\neq i
,
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}}

is not
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
-maximal in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathbb{W}_{j}}
, then there exists

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}<_{w}w^{\prime}_{j}}
. This implies

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{j}}
by
Fig.

5
and since

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
extends
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}
, which by

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i},w_{j}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{j}}
lets us
construct
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{wrDiff}}$}}}$}}}w^{\prime}_{j}}
, which implies

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}<_{w}w^{\prime}_{j}}
, however this contradicts maximality of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
. Thus each
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{j}}
is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
-maximal in

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathbb{W}_{j}}
, meaning
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
is a maximal candidate.

(4)

If there exists
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
with

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
not being
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}
-maximal, then there must exist
some other event
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}
such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s\vartriangleleft e}
. If there
exists multiple scans observing
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
we let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
be
the
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}
-greatest among them. If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}
is a write event

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{j}}
then we have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{j}}
since

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{j}}
, which contradicts
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
being
the greatest write in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
. If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}
is a scan

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s^{\prime}}
then we either have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s^{\prime}}
or

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s^{\prime}}
for some write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}}\neq{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
,
however the former contradicts our selection of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
since
both
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s^{\prime}}
observe
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
, but

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s\vartriangleleft s^{\prime}}
contradicts that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
be the

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}
-greatest among scans observing
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
. We thus
have that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s^{\prime}}
and since we also have

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\vartriangleleft s^{\prime}}
by
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s\vartriangleleft s^{\prime}}
, if
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}

is in-between
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s^{\prime}}
we contradict

Fig.

5
, thus we have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\not\vartriangleleft w_{i}}

which by
Fig.

5
we must have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}}
,
which contradicts that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
is
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
-maximal.
∎

To construct the total order, we recursively select a maximal
candidate, which we make the last event of the total order and remove
from consecutive steps.

Theorem A.5.

Every history satisfying our snapshot specifications is
linearizable, i.e., there exists a total order
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<}
over each
event which forms a legal sequential snapshot behavior and extends

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}
.

Proof.

By induction on the number of events in the history. The minimal
history, the one that only consists of the initial writes, is
clearly linearizable, no matter what order
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
we have.

Our inductive hypothesis is that for some history

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E\subseteq E_{c}}
satisfying the axioms under
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}
,
there exists a total order
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<}
over
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E}
that forms a
legal sequential snapshot behavior and extends
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}
when only considering events in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E}
. For the
inductive step, we need to prove for a there exists such a total
order
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<^{\prime}}
of history
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E^{\prime}}
, which extends
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E}
by
the single event
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}
, with
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}
being an arbitrary
maximal candidate of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
under history
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E^{\prime}}
. By

Lemma

A.4
, since
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E^{\prime}}
is non-empty, there
must exist a maximal candidate. Consider the two following cases for

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
is a write. By the definition of being a
maximal candidate, we know that there exists no scan in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E^{\prime}}

observing
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
. If we remove
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
from
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E^{\prime}}

then the new history
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E}
still satisfies the snapshot
properties since it is safe to remove any event that is not read
visible. By the inductive hypothesis we have a total order

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<}
over
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E}
that forms a legal snapshot behavior and
extends
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}
. To construct the total order

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<^{\prime}}
over
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E^{\prime}}
, we extend
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<}
with write

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
being the last event in the order. Since any write
event added to the end of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<^{\prime}}
cannot influence the result
of previous scan events, it means that since
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<}
forms a
legal snapshot behavior, so must
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<^{\prime}}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
is a scan. By the definition of being a
maximal candidate, we know that all the writes
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
observe
are maximal under
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
in their respective memory cell. If
we remove
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
from
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E^{\prime}}
then the new history
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E}

still satisfies the snapshot properties since no other events
depend on scans. By the inductive hypothesis we have a total order

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<}
over
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E}
that forms legal snapshot behavior and
extends
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\vartriangleleft}
. To construct the total order

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<^{\prime}}
over
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}E^{\prime}}
, we extend
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<}
with scan

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
being the last event in the order. The
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<^{\prime}}
order
forms a legal snapshot behavior, since any write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
that

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
observes is maximal in
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<_{w}}
, it is impossible
for another write to be in-between
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
, thus
since
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<}
forms a legal snapshot behavior, so does

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}<^{\prime}}
. ∎

Appendix B
Forwarding Signature Implies Snapshot Signature

We here give the full proof that any history satisfying the
\mathsf{Forwarding}

signature (
Fig.

7
) also satisfies the
\mathsf{Snapshot}

signature (
Fig.

5
). We start by determining the
instantiations of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathbb{W}_{i}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\prec}

for the
\mathsf{Snapshot}
signature. Let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}\in{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathbb{W}_{i}}

iff
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
has executed
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
, since the effect of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
is only observable after this execution. We define a
helper notion of visibility over scans
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{sc}}$}}}$}}}}
, which we use to
define
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\prec}
. The highlighted relations
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{rf}$}}$}}}$}}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{wr}$}}$}}}$}}}}
originate from the
\mathsf{Forwarding}
signature.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\quad\triangleq}\hphantom{{}\implies{}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{rf}$}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{sc}}$}}}$}}}s^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\quad\triangleq}\hphantom{{}\implies{}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}\sqsubset{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s^{\prime}}^{\bullet}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\prec}

\displaystyle\mathrlap{\quad\triangleq}\hphantom{{}\implies{}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{wr}$}}$}}}$}}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{sc}}$}}}$}}}}

We prove the
\mathsf{Snapshot}
properties by unfolding the definition of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}
in the properties so that we can apply the
\mathsf{Forwarding}

properties.

Fig.

5
0.

For every terminated scan
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
and index
i
, there exists some
write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
such that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}.\mathsf{in}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}.\mathsf{out}[i]
.

Proof.

By
Fig.

7
, there exists
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
such as

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{rf}$}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}.\mathsf{in}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}.\mathsf{out}[i]
for
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}
, where the
latter immediately corresponds to one of our goals and the former
corresponds to the remaining goal by the instantiation of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}}
giving us

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{rf}$}}$}}}$}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.4,0}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}s}^{\bullet}}}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
.
∎

Fig.

5
0.

Every terminated write is effectful, i.e.,

\mathcal{T}({\color[rgb]{1,0,1}W_{i}})\subseteq{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathbb{W}_{i}}
.

Proof.

Since every terminated event must have executed all their rep
events, it follows that a terminated write
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
must have
executed
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
, which matches the instantiation of

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathbb{W}_{i}}
.
∎

Fig.

5
0.

For two distinct writes
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}}
, we have either

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}}
or
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\vartriangleleft w_{i}}
.

Proof.

For each write, we either have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
or

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
by
Fig.

3
. By the
definition of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{wr}$}}$}}}$}}}}
, if we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
then we have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{wr}$}}$}}}$}}}w^{\prime}_{i}}

(and thus also
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\vartriangleleft w^{\prime}_{i}}
) and if we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.6}{w^{\prime}_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}\vartriangleleft{w_{i}}\hskip-1.0pt{\mathit{a}}}
then we have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{wr}$}}$}}}$}}}w_{i}}

(and thus also
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w^{\prime}_{i}\vartriangleleft w_{i}}
).
∎

Eq.

V.1
0.

If
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e\prec^{+}e^{\prime}}
then we cannot have

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e^{\prime}\sqsubseteq e}
.

Proof.

Assume we have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e^{\prime}\sqsubseteq e}
, we derive a contradiction.
Consider first that we have
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e=e^{\prime}}
such that we have

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e\prec^{+}e}
. There is no
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}w_{i}\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{rf}}$}}}$}}}s}
in

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e\prec^{+}e}
since if there is, there is no way to relate from
a scan back to a write, i.e., there is no observation from scan to
write. Thus we must have either
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\hbox{\pagecolor{lightergray}$\displaystyle\scriptstyle\mathbf{wr}$}}$}}}$}}}^{+}e}
or

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}e\mathrel{\raisebox{-2.0pt}{{$\xrightarrow{{\raisebox{-2.0pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\scriptstyle{\mathbf{sc}}$}}}$}}}^{+}e}
